The past weeks have seen the broad crypto ETF market face consistent withdrawals from investors, causing Bitcoin, Ethereum, XRP, Solana, and others to record steady outflows since October.

While the negative trend has continued, Solana has just taken over the leadership in the broad ETF market after recording its first inflow in a while.

Latest data from SosoValue shows that while Bitcoin, Ethereum, and XRP ETFs have continued to struggle with consistent weekly outflows, Solana has just beaten the trend after it finally ended a six-week streak of consecutive weekly outflows.

Notably, the data shows that Solana spot ETFs have recorded a net inflow of $4.38 million over the week.

Although the net amount of capital received by the ETFs appears decent, it is significant considering the growing crypto market weakness.

Further data shows that Bitcoin spot ETFs suffered the largest withdrawals as investors pulled out about $1.79 billion during the same period.

The negative trend also extended to the Ethereum ETF market as the funds posted a total of $314.17 million in net outflows following the consistent selling pressure.

In addition to this, the XRP spot ETFs were not spared, as the funds recorded $11.11 million in weekly outflows, leaving Solana as the only one among the four major crypto ETFs to finish the week on a positive note.

#5024. Monad faces $190 million insider token unlock larger than its market cap as MON hits record low

The 16.61 billion MON release equals 140.5% of circulating supply, threatening fresh selling pressure and a potential shake-up in validator voting power.

Monad is preparing to unlock 16.61 billion MON tokens on Nov. 24, exceeding its circulating supply as the cryptocurrency hits record lows.

The release represents 140.5% of the 11.83 billion MON currently circulating and marks the first major insider distribution since the blockchain launched in November 2025.

According to DefiLlama, approximately 10.7 billion MON will go to team members, 4.925 billion to investors, and 987.5 million to the Category Labs treasury. This means its team and investor allocations account for 94% of the release.

The unlock was valued at approximately $226 million in Tokenomist's earlier estimate, but MON's decline has reduced its value to roughly $190 million, still exceeding the token's $137 million market capitalization.

CryptoSlate's data shows MON plunged around 11% over the past 24 hours, touching an all-time low of $0.01152 ahead of the release. It is trading at $0.012 as of press time.

The incoming supply adds to existing market pressure, with further insider distributions scheduled through 2029.

Historical data from Alea Research shows that 16 comparable token unlocks recorded median declines of 25.2% in the preceding month and 6.9% in the following month.

Category Labs has authorized up to $80 million in MON repurchases through Dec. 31, although Alea found no disclosed purchases under the program.

Beyond potential selling, the release could alter how Monad distributes voting power and staking rewards.

The network had 20.73 billion MON backing validator votes across 197 operators on Nov. 22, according to Valopers.

If insiders stake just 2.3 billion MON, approximately 14% of their newly available allocation, those tokens would account for 10% of the resulting voting power, assuming existing stake remains unchanged.

At 25% participation, approximately 4.15 billion MON would enter active staking, representing 16.7% of the expanded total.

That scenario would also reduce gross inflationary rewards per staked MON by 16.7%, assuming issuance and validator commissions remain constant.

Three scenarios model additions to active stake from the estimated 16.613 billion MON release scheduled for November 24.

If a quarter of the estimated release enters active stake, the new MON would represent 16.7% of the enlarged total.

All three scenarios assume the displayed amounts become active stake and the existing stake stays fixed. They model aggregate voting weight after activation. Actual delegations and recipient validators can vary.

Start with 20.73 billion MON of Valopers voting power in epoch 2,491 and a 16.613 billion MON release estimate from DefiLlama. Multiply the release by 10%, 25% and 50%, then divide each addition by the baseline plus that addition. Bar lengths use a common 0–30 billion MON scale. Amounts are rounded to two decimals and shares to one.

All three scenarios assume the displayed amounts become active stake and the existing stake stays fixed. They model aggregate voting weight after activation. Actual delegations and recipient validators can vary.

The potential redistribution comes amid substantial concentration of Foundation-linked staking allocations.

Alea estimated that Foundation-linked wallets accounted for 78% of active stake on Nov. 1, while Backpack controlled 10.82% of validator voting weight on Nov. 22.

Monad initially prohibited staking locked insider tokens to direct more early rewards toward public holders. The restriction expires with the unlock, allowing newly eligible tokens to compete for staking rewards and validator influence.

The first post-unlock delegations will show whether insiders reinforce the largest validators or spread voting power across smaller operators, while trading activity will reveal how much of the new supply reaches exchanges.

#5023. Bitcoin Reserves Just Found a New Use on Wall Street, but There’s a Catch

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Bitcoin reserves have found another potential use within the US financial system. The Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) has clarified that regulated derivatives exchanges and clearinghouses can count Bitcoin, Ethereum and qualifying payment stablecoins toward certain financial resource requirements. However, the regulator is not prepared to treat those holdings like cash.

The guidance, issued on November 23, could give regulated trading venues greater flexibility in managing their balance sheets. For Bitcoin investors, however, the more interesting question is whether that flexibility will translate into fresh institutional demand.

The answer is not quite as straightforward as it might seem.

The CFTC’s latest advisory addresses three types of regulated businesses: swap execution facilities, designated contract markets and derivatives clearing organizations.

These institutions are required to maintain sufficient financial resources to cover at least one year of operating costs. Under the new staff guidance, Bitcoin, Ether and qualifying payment stablecoins may contribute toward those requirements, subject to appropriate valuation discounts.

The announcement follows broader efforts to integrate digital assets into regulated financial infrastructure. NewsBTC previously reported on the CFTC’s guidance concerning tokenized collateral at clearinghouses, another area where traditional financial markets are gradually accommodating blockchain-based assets.

This time, the discussion is not about what traders can post as collateral. It is about the assets that the institutions running those markets can rely on to keep their businesses operating.

For a company that already holds Bitcoin, the clarification could make those holdings more useful from a regulatory standpoint. For businesses considering adding cryptocurrency to their balance sheets, it removes some uncertainty surrounding how those assets would be recognized.

But that recognition comes at a discount!

The CFTC advisory references a minimum 20% valuation haircut for Bitcoin and Ethereum, consistent with earlier staff treatment of proprietary cryptocurrency positions held by futures commission merchants.

Qualifying payment stablecoins receive considerably more favorable treatment, with a referenced haircut of 2%.

An exchange holding $10 million in Bitcoin, for example, would be able to count $8 million toward the relevant financial resource requirement if it applied a 20% haircut and no further deductions were necessary.

The remaining $2 million is not a realized loss. It is a regulatory adjustment intended to account for the risk associated with holding a volatile asset.

Crypto assets that are neither readily marketable nor qualifying payment stablecoins would generally face a 100% haircut, effectively excluding them from the calculation.

Perhaps the most revealing part of the advisory is the regulator’s warning about what it calls wrong-way risk.

A DCM should consider potential wrong-way risk

For a cryptocurrency-focused exchange, that risk is particularly relevant. A prolonged Bitcoin sell-off could put pressure on trading revenues while simultaneously reducing the value of the assets the exchange relies on to support its operations.

The problem is not simply that Bitcoin is volatile. It is that its value could deteriorate at precisely the moment a crypto business finds itself under financial pressure.

A 20% valuation discount provides some protection against that scenario, but it is hardly a guarantee.

Despite allowing digital assets to contribute toward broader operating resources, the CFTC has maintained a separate requirement for cash and highly liquid securities.

Bitcoin, Ethereum and even qualifying payment stablecoins cannot be used to satisfy that particular obligation under the advisory.

Swap execution facilities must maintain qualifying liquid resources sufficient to cover at least three months of projected operating expenses or the cost of winding down their operations, whichever is greater.

For designated contract markets and derivatives clearing organizations, the operating-cost liquidity requirement generally covers at least six months.

The distinction matters because an institution can have substantial assets on its balance sheet without necessarily having the cash available to meet immediate obligations.

It is also worth noting that the guidance does not address whether cryptocurrencies can satisfy the separate financial resources that clearinghouses must maintain to cover clearing-member defaults.

In effect, the CFTC is recognizing Bitcoin as an asset that can contribute to an institution’s financial strength without giving it the same regulatory standing as cash.

That is a more cautious approach than some Bitcoin advocates might have hoped for, but arguably a more sustainable one for integrating cryptocurrency into financial infrastructure.

The bullish interpretation is relatively simple. If regulated financial institutions can derive greater utility from holding Bitcoin, more of them may eventually consider allocating capital to the cryptocurrency.

The expansion of regulated crypto infrastructure has already brought digital assets closer to traditional financial markets. Earlier, NewsBTC reported on Coinbase’s CFTC registration for its USDC-focused derivatives clearinghouse, illustrating how cryptocurrency businesses are increasingly operating within established regulatory frameworks.

The latest advisory gives relevant institutions another reason to evaluate how digital assets fit into their balance sheets.

Still, there is a considerable difference between allowing an institution to hold Bitcoin for regulatory purposes and giving it an economic incentive to buy more.

An exchange that already owns Bitcoin could benefit from the clarification without purchasing a single additional coin. Another might conclude that holding conventional securities remains more efficient, particularly given the valuation discounts and separate liquidity requirements.

Nothing in the advisory requires institutions to acquire Bitcoin, and no purchase commitments were disclosed alongside the announcement.

Investors should therefore resist the temptation to translate this development directly into a bullish price forecast.

The longer-term significance may be greater than the immediate market impact. Every additional recognized use for Bitcoin within regulated financial markets strengthens the case for its utility beyond speculative trading.

However, the November 23 document remains a staff advisory rather than a binding rule adopted by the full Commission. It does not amend existing regulations or create enforceable rights.

The next meaningful development will be evidence that regulated institutions are actually changing their reserve allocations in response.

Until then, Bitcoin has gained another form of regulatory recognition. Whether it gains another source of sustained buying pressure is a different question entirely.

#5021. Peter Brandt Softens Bitcoin (BTC) Warning as Price Rebounds to $63,000

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Veteran trader Peter Brandt has softened the tone of his recent Bitcoin warnings and said on X that the cryptocurrency held at the level it needed to hold after its drop to $55,150 over the weekend.

"Bitcoin held exactly where it had to hold -- for now anyway, but perhaps for more than now," Brandt wrote on Monday.

Brandt also shared a monthly chart of Bitcoin together with the post. The chart goes back to 2018, and Brandt marked on it the same patterns that he had already pointed to in previous years. According to the chart, the price of Bitcoin was back above $63,000 at the time of the post and stood at $63,338.

On Saturday, Bitcoin fell as low as $55,150. It was the lowest price of the cryptocurrency since September 2024, and before that, the lowest price of Bitcoin in 2026 was $57,718 on July 1, according to Coinbase.

On Nov. 15, Brandt posted a weekly chart with a possible head-and-shoulders top and said that, if the pattern proves valid, Bitcoin's October 2025 record of about $126,000 may not be surpassed in his lifetime. Two days before that, on Nov. 13, Bitcoin dropped below $70,000 for the first time since August.

Brandt has also pointed to the history of Bitcoin's bull market cycles more than once. Late last year, he posted a chart showing that each of Bitcoin's five major bull runs began after drawdowns of 75% or more, and he wrote that if the decline carried to $50,000, "the next bull market cycle should carry to $200k to $250K."

In April, Brandt said that Bitcoin could form an "investable low" in September or October 2026. According to Brandt, this low may or may not go below the February low near $60,000, and the next cycle peak could land between $300,000 and $500,000 in September or October 2029 if the four-year cycle continues to hold.

"Real bottom will not occur until October 2026," Brandt said in an interview in February.

The latest Brandt post comes amid a rally in U.S. tech stocks on Monday. Earlier that day, Nvidia announced that its board voted unanimously to reinstate Jensen Huang as president, chief executive officer and board member, effective immediately.

According to the Nvidia announcement, the board received input from senior levels of the U.S. government before the decision, and it is confident that Huang's return "poses no national security risk." Shares of Nvidia surged 33% after the news, according to CNBC.

Bitcoin went up together with tech stocks and briefly climbed above $64,000 on Monday. Before the Nvidia news, Bitcoin had already recovered from the Saturday low to around $61,500.

Huang resigned from Nvidia on Nov. 12. On Nov. 17, Nvidia board chair Harvey Jones said on the company's earnings call that the board "does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

#5020. XRP ETF Shares Surface in Schwab’s Repo Collateral

Home » Trendsetter News » XRP ETF Shares Surface in Schwab’s Repo Collateral

XRP ETF shares just appeared in collateral baskets pledged by JPMorgan & Bank of America to a Schwab money market fund.

XRP exchange-traded fund shares appeared in collateral baskets posted by JPMorgan and Bank of America to a Schwab money market fund, according to wealth coach Kamilah Stevenson.

In her latest YouTube episode, she argues that the reported holdings point to a less visible source of institutional demand: securities pledged against loans rather than bought for price exposure.

The distinction matters. Stevenson says Schwab did not buy XRP or the ETFs outright; its fund lent cash and received baskets of securities backing the transactions.

Citing monthly SEC disclosures, Stevenson identifies XRP ETF shares from Grayscale, Canary, Franklin and Bitwise among collateral supplied by the banks’ trading arms.

She puts their combined value at roughly $36,000 in April, $2 million in June and more than $8 million by August, an increase exceeding 200-fold.

Those figures remain claims presented in the video. The transcript does not specify the reporting year or filing identifiers, limiting verification of the sequence.

Stevenson acknowledges that the positions were small relative to the underlying loans. Their inclusion would demonstrate acceptance of particular ETF securities within collateral baskets, not necessarily an institutional commitment to holding XRP directly.

The YouTube video episode connects those reported positions to BlackRock’s effort to expand acceptance of tokenized fund shares as collateral. Dr. Stevenson says the appeal is straightforward: investors could pledge income-producing fund shares instead of redeeming them to provide cash.

She also points to Binance’s acceptance of BlackRock’s BUIDL fund as collateral and describes a wider shift toward blockchain-based collateral transfers. Her thesis is that assets used to secure financing can generate more persistent demand than assets held solely for trading.

“Trading creates excitement; collateral creates demand that stays,” Dr. Stevenson says.

That argument needs a boundary. Acceptance of XRP ETF shares does not establish that lenders accept XRP itself, and tokenized collateral does not automatically require XRP to move between markets. Stevenson presents direct XRP-backed lending and cross-currency settlement as potential next steps, not completed developments.

#5019. Binance Hack: US Moves to Recover $1.1 Billion, but Who Gets the Money?

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Nearly four weeks after the Binance hack, US authorities have filed a civil forfeiture complaint seeking more than $1.1 billion in USDC linked to the October theft. The money is already subject to restrictions, but the latest court filings reveal a complication: freezing stolen cryptocurrency is considerably easier than returning it.

The complaint, filed on November 23, in the US District Court for the District of Columbia, targets USDC associated with 36 cryptocurrency addresses across 18 blockchains.

Federal investigators attribute the October 29 attack to North Korean hacking groups, including the Lazarus Group. The complaint puts the value of the original theft at approximately $6 billion.

For Binance, the latest legal action represents another opportunity to recover a portion of its losses. For the wider cryptocurrency industry, however, the case raises questions about the authority stablecoin issuers exercise over digital assets and what happens when governments become involved.

The assets targeted by US authorities are not an entirely new discovery.

Earlier this month, NewsBTC reported that Circle had frozen approximately $1.1 billion in USDC linked to the Binance hack, following cooperation with law enforcement.

What has changed is the government’s attempt to establish a legal basis for taking possession of those assets.

According to the court documents, US authorities obtained a temporary restraining order on November 12. Prosecutors subsequently requested that the restrictions remain in place throughout the civil forfeiture proceedings.

Both Binance and Circle consented to the initial restraining order and the requested extension, according to the government’s November 23 application.

The agreement between the companies and federal authorities reduces one immediate obstacle to preserving the funds. It does not, however, resolve the question of their ultimate ownership or distribution.

Civil forfeiture proceedings are directed against property believed to be connected to criminal activity. Interested parties can assert legitimate claims to that property through the legal process.

In this case, prosecutors are seeking forfeiture of the assets to the United States. That is not the same thing as a court ordering their immediate return to Binance.

Perhaps the most interesting disclosure in the complaint concerns Circle itself.

The stablecoin issuer can restrict transfers involving USDC held at particular blockchain addresses. However, that authority does not necessarily give Circle direct control over the wallets holding those tokens.

does not have the technical ability to transfer the Defendant Property from the blocklisted Subject Addresses to the United States

Instead, Circle has agreed to issue an equivalent amount of replacement USDC and transfer those tokens to US authorities upon receiving a forfeiture order from the court.

The proposed arrangement exposes an unusual feature of centralized stablecoins. An issuer can prevent tokens from circulating without possessing the private keys needed to move them.

It also raises an accounting question that deserves attention. If replacement tokens are issued, what ultimately happens to the original frozen USDC, and how will Circle ensure that the treatment of those balances is reflected accurately in its outstanding supply and reserve reporting?

The issuance of replacement tokens would not, by itself, prove that USDC had become undercollateralized. Nevertheless, transparency around the implementation would be important for a stablecoin whose credibility depends on confidence in its backing.

The immediate concern is not whether Circle can create new tokens. It is whether the entire recovery process can be independently accounted for.

The temptation to describe the Justice Department’s announcement as a billion-dollar recovery is understandable. It is also premature.

There are three separate stages involved: restricting access to the stolen assets, obtaining legal authority over them and determining how they will ultimately be distributed.

The November 23 filings advance the second stage. They do not establish that the third has been completed.

There is also a cross-border complication.

On November 18, Reuters reported that an Abu Dhabi court had directed several cryptocurrency companies, including Circle, to preserve digital assets associated with the Binance theft.

The involvement of authorities in both the United States and the United Arab Emirates illustrates how a single cryptocurrency theft can generate legal proceedings across multiple jurisdictions.

Blockchain transactions may cross national borders without much difficulty. Establishing ownership rights over the assets involved is a considerably less straightforward exercise.

That does not mean the US and UAE proceedings are necessarily in conflict. But the ultimate disposition of the funds will require more than an announcement from either government.

For investors assessing Binance’s financial position, the distinction between frozen assets and recovered capital is particularly important. Restricted cryptocurrency should not automatically be treated as money already returned to the exchange.

For years, the ability of stablecoin issuers to freeze tokens has been criticized by advocates of decentralized finance.

The Binance case demonstrates the other side of that argument.

Without Circle’s ability to restrict transactions, preserving such a large amount of cryptocurrency for potential recovery would have been more difficult.

In this particular case, centralized control has provided a mechanism that may help prevent suspected criminals from benefiting from stolen assets.

The uncomfortable question is whether the cryptocurrency industry can benefit from that authority while also demanding meaningful safeguards against its misuse.

The answer depends partly on transparency. Token holders should be able to understand why assets were frozen, under what legal authority restrictions remain in place and how disputes over ownership are resolved.

The same standard should apply to the exchange, the stablecoin issuer and the government.

The next significant development in the Binance case will be the court’s treatment of the forfeiture claim and any competing claims to the assets. Circle’s proposed issuance of replacement USDC will also deserve scrutiny if it proceeds.

Until then, the $1.1 billion should be understood as assets subject to an ongoing recovery effort, not a completed repayment.

For a cryptocurrency industry that frequently celebrates the speed of moving money, the Binance hack is a reminder that getting it back can be an entirely different business.

#5018. Bitcoin Bloodbath Fails to Stop Michael Saylor's Buying Spree

Despite the sharp fall in the cryptocurrency market, Michael Saylor-led Strategy has not stopped its Bitcoin (BTC) buying spree, as it picked up 457 BTC worth around $29.6 million.

The firm's recent purchase comes as the leading cryptocurrency has been struggling to rebound from the heavy sell-off that recently dragged it to a two-plus-year low.

The latest data from Strategy's SEC filing reveals that the firm has bought 457 Bitcoin at an average price of $64,898 per coin.

The new purchase brings Strategy’s Bitcoin holdings to around 851,305 BTC, which accounts for over 4% of the cryptocurrency's maximum supply of 21 million coins.

The firm has now spent around $64.23 billion to build its Bitcoin holdings at an average price of $75,446 per Bitcoin.

It is worth noting that Strategy has been buying BTC at gradually decreasing prices as the cryptocurrency market continues to bleed heavily.

On Nov. 2, the firm announced that it had bought 811 BTC, worth around $65.5 million, at an average price of $80,846 per Bitcoin.

A week later, on Nov. 9, Strategy announced the purchase of 1,036 BTC worth $81.9 million at an average price of $79,012.

The firm’s Bitcoin purchase a week later, on Nov. 16, was relatively smaller at 485 BTC, worth $33.4 million, with an average price of $68,887.

The new 457 BTC purchase continues this pattern. The latest Strategy purchase was made at an almost 20% lower price than the firm’s Nov. 2 acquisition.

Overall, the firm has made four weekly purchases, scooping up around 2,789 BTC worth $210.4 million.

However, these purchases have been much smaller than before. The latest $29.6 million purchase is almost 64% lower than the $81.9 million purchase announced just two weeks ago.

Although Saylor is doubling down on Bitcoin, Strategy’s massive holdings in the cryptocurrency are underwater as the market has been struggling to regain its footing.

On Nov. 21, Bitcoin hit a low of around $55,130, its lowest level since September 2024, before rebounding above $61,000.

The firm’s recent purchases have helped reduce its overall average Bitcoin acquisition cost, but the drop has not been that significant.

For example, with the latest purchase, the average cost of the firm’s Bitcoin holdings dropped from $75,452 to around $75,446, a decline of just $6 per Bitcoin.

At the same time, the firm has also been putting a lot of resources into supporting its preferred securities.

In the latest reporting period, Strategy sold around 1.93 million common shares, raising net proceeds of $176.7 million.

#5017. Bitcoin didn’t crash 70%, but Kraken’s order book did – Here’s what went wrong

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Crypto exchange Kraken recently faced a flash crash that was caused by a liquidity shortage in one of its Canadian-dollar trading markets, rather than an actual crash in Bitcoin [BTC]’s global market price.

Over the weekend, Bitcoin’s price on Kraken’s BTC/CAD trading pair suddenly dropped to C$24,250. This was equivalent to approximately $16,900 at the prevailing USD/CAD exchange rate.

This came as a shock because Bitcoin was trading around $57,650 on Kraken’s BTC/USD market during that period. In Bitcoin trading at nearly 70% below its price on other markets.

However, this was not a market-wide Bitcoin crash, as other major exchanges continued to show Bitcoin trading between $55,000 and $60,000.

The primary suspected reason was a sudden shortage of liquidity in Kraken’s BTC/CAD order book. This liquidity shortage, aka liquidity vacuum, is what caused a sudden price crash without Bitcoin’s actual market value declining.

Expressing their opinion on the matter, Blockstream CEO Adam Back suggested that the disruption may have originated from a market maker running out of funds in Kraken’s USD/CAD market.

As expected, this distortion may have spread to other CAD trading pairs through arbitrage, where traders buy assets at lower prices and sell them elsewhere at higher prices.

However, Kraken has not confirmed this explanation, yet. The exchange maintained that all orders were processed correctly and refused to reverse completed trades.

This followed the exchange experiencing a one-hour technical outage on 30 October 2026, affecting its website, app, and trading APIs.

But at the same time, Kraken recently expanded its xStocks platform to over 1,000 tokenized stock listings, according to AMBCrypto’s previous report.

#5016. Strategy raises its buyback ceiling to $4 billion as MSTR shares sink 21%

The company has spent nearly $2.9 billion retiring preferred shares, while analysts question the economics of funding those purchases with common-stock issuance.

Strategy raised its STRC buyback authorization for the third time on Monday, bringing the program to $4 billion as its common shares continued to struggle.

The Bitcoin treasury company had exhausted nearly $2.88 billion, or nearly 96% of its previous $3 billion authorization, after spending another $211.8 million repurchasing STRC during the week ended Nov. 22.

The latest expansion comes as Strategy's common shares have fallen 21.1% since it announced its first STRC repurchases on July 27. MSTR closed at $77.80 on Nov. 20, compared with $98.65 nearly four months earlier.

Strategy launched its initial $1 billion digital credit repurchase program in June, then increased it to $2 billion in September and $3 billion in October. Monday's announcement marks the third consecutive $1 billion expansion, bringing cumulative expansion to $4 billion.

The growing commitment has coincided with a shift in how Strategy deploys money raised from common shareholders.

According to its latest SEC filing, the company issued 1.93 million MSTR shares last week, raising $176.7 million.

Of those proceeds, $147 million (83.2%) funded STRC repurchases, while only $29.6 million (16.8%) went toward acquiring 457 Bitcoin.

Strategy contributed another $64.8 million from existing cash to complete the $211.8 million preferred-share repurchase.

Weekly repurchase spending in US dollars, reported amounts are rounded to $0.1M.

Source: Strategy Form 8-K, Nov. 23, 2026 · STRC Live historical buyback ledger

The spending has helped reduce its future dividend obligations but has not kept STRC consistently near its $100 stated amount. The security closed at $93.60 on Nov. 20, despite Strategy maintaining a 12% annual dividend rate to support its price.

Meanwhile, Strategy retains a separate $1 billion authorization to repurchase MSTR shares, which remained entirely unused last week.

Bitcoin analyst Derin Olenik criticized the continued issuance of common shares to finance preferred-share repurchases, arguing the transactions weaken shareholders' underlying Bitcoin exposure.

Strategy's published figures support a decline in gross Bitcoin holdings per assumed diluted share during the latest reporting week.

Its outstanding Bitcoin balance increased from 850,849 BTC on Nov. 15 to 851,305 BTC on Nov. 22, while assumed diluted shares outstanding rose from approximately 457.24 million to 459.19 million.

Consequently, gross Bitcoin per share declined from approximately 186,082 satoshis to 185,392 satoshis, a reduction of about 690 satoshis (0.37%).

However, Strategy executive chairman Michael Saylor defended the transactions, arguing that Strategy's approach has consistently increased shareholders' net Bitcoin exposure despite the dilution.

“Our trades accreted Net BPS in each of the last 13 weeks. Cumulatively, we increased Net BPS by 584 sats/share and generated a Net BTC Gain of 2,505 BTC or $199M before preferred dividends, measured at a fixed $BTC price each week.”

Strategy-reported positive weekly contributions to net BTC per common share (in satoshis) before preferred dividends and holding BTC price fixed within each week. Values are transcribed from the supplied Strategy-branded Nov. 23 chart at one-decimal precision.

Source: Strategy net BPS methodology; individual values from supplied Strategy-branded chart · Strategy, November 23, 2026 corporate disclosure · Strategy, November 19 commentary on previous 12 weeks

Saylor also reported that the latest week's transactions increased Net Bitcoin Per Share by six satoshis and improved Strategy's Net Reserve by $183 million.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The conflicting figures reflect competing assessments of Strategy's capital allocation. Gross Bitcoin per share measures its holdings against diluted shares, while Strategy's Net Bitcoin Per Share accounts for senior claims and dollar assets when calculating the Bitcoin exposure attributable to common shareholders.

Under Saylor's framework, retiring preferred securities can increase net exposure even when common-share issuance reduces gross Bitcoin holdings per share.

Olenik remained critical of the economics, questioning whether the increasingly complex transactions justify the continued dilution of common shareholders.

He also challenged the economics of STRC repurchases. His calculations put the security's average issuance price at $95.81 and its average repurchase price at $97.93, meaning Strategy has spent more buying back preferred shares than it originally received per share issued.

Average price estimated by dividing reported weekly spending, rounded to $0.1M, by shares repurchased. Per-share cents are approximate.

Source: Strategy Form 8-K, Nov. 23, 2026 · STRC Live historical buyback ledger

Strategy has defended repurchases below STRC's $100 stated amount because retiring the securities reduces future dividend obligations.

Saylor said Strategy has reduced outstanding credit by $2.9 billion in recent months, lowering annual dividend obligations by approximately $353 million.

The latest transactions alone retired 2.18 million STRC shares, eliminating approximately $26.2 million in annualized dividends at the current 12% rate.

However, the company's latest decision suggests that reducing those obligations will remain a major capital priority.

Alongside the third buyback expansion, Strategy authorized transferring $1 billion from its dedicated USD Reserve into discretionary USD Cash, which management can use for further repurchases or Bitcoin purchases.

The change marks a departure from its original July policy, which explicitly prohibited using the USD Reserve to fund STRC repurchases.

Saylor said the reallocation was possible because the company's remaining reserve could cover its dividend and interest obligations for 3.4 years.

Following the adjustment, Strategy reported approximately $3.7 billion in dedicated reserves and over $1 billion in discretionary cash, leaving total dollar assets at roughly $4.8 billion.

Olenik interpreted the move as evidence that Strategy was preparing to rely more heavily on existing liquidity when issuing MSTR shares becomes less attractive.

That would let Strategy continue retiring preferred securities without relying entirely on new common-share issuance, although the company has not committed the reallocated cash to a specific purchase.

Strategy now has $1.1 billion in remaining preferred-share buyback authority, nearly matching its discretionary cash balance.

Its next capital-allocation decision will determine whether the expanded financial resources support further STRC repurchases or reverse the recent pattern of relatively modest Bitcoin purchases and continued MSTR dilution.

The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

On Monday, XRP briefly rose above $1 after the return of Jensen Huang to Nvidia sparked a rally on Wall Street and a wave of short liquidations in the crypto market. At the peak of the move, short liquidations on XRP exceeded long liquidations by 578%, according to CoinGlass data.

On the XRPUSDT perpetual contract on Binance, the price of XRP went from $0.94 to as high as $1.02 within minutes after 14:00 UTC, and later it pulled back and stood at $0.984, according to TradingView.

Earlier on Monday, before the opening bell, Nvidia announced that its board voted unanimously to reinstate Huang as president, chief executive and board member, effective immediately. The company said that the board was satisfied with Huang's "candor and ability to lead" after a series of meetings with him, and that its independent investigation would continue. According to the company, the board received input from senior U.S. government officials and concluded that Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

"The Board believes Jensen is the right person to lead NVIDIA, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers," Nvidia board chair Harvey Jones said in a statement.

Huang resigned as CEO on Nov. 12, and on Nov. 17, during the company's earnings call, Jones said that the board "does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

In premarket trading, Nvidia stock jumped 27% to $159.48 as of 9 a.m. ET, and at the start of U.S. trading on Monday, Nvidia shares were up more than 30%. The rally spread across Wall Street, and the S&P 500 rose as much as 5.6% and the Nasdaq 100 as much as 7.9%, according to NBC News.

CoinGlass data shows that $9.45 million worth of XRP positions were liquidated over the past 24 hours, and $7.18 million of that amount came from short positions. Long positions accounted for $2.27 million.

The same happened across the rest of the crypto market. In total, 110,663 traders were liquidated for $660.89 million over the past 24 hours, according to CoinGlass, and shorts accounted for $515.94 million of that amount.

Bitcoin led the list with $273.26 million in liquidations, and 82.8% of that amount came from short positions. Ethereum followed with $139.07 million, and Solana was third with $24.93 million.

Besides, perpetual contracts on Nvidia stock and on SOXL, a triple-leveraged exchange-traded fund on semiconductor stocks, were next on the list with $16.34 million and $15.01 million in liquidations, respectively, according to CoinGlass.

#5014. Bitcoin ETF flows turn negative for the second straight November — What’s changed?

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Spot Bitcoin [BTC] ETFs have been facing a tough time thanks to consecutive days of outflows. According to SoSo Value data, U.S Spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $669 million in net outflows on 20th November, marking their fourth consecutive day of outflows.

That said, in 2026, it was only in the months of August, September, and April that Bitcoin ETFs attracted $3.52 billion, $2.65 billion, and $1.97 billion in inflows, respectively.

On the contrary, the month of June recorded $4.51 billion in outflows, while May saw $2.43 billion leave these investment products. November has recorded approximately $4.14 billion in net outflows so far.

November flows in Bitcoin Spot ETFs have swung sharply across the three years. For instance, November 2024 was the strongest, with net inflows of about $6.49 billion during the post-election rally, lifting cumulative inflows from $24.2 billion to $30.7 billion. However, November 2025 reversed to net outflows of 3.48 billion, while November 2026 has so far been worse. That hinted at a swing of roughly $10.6 billion from Nov 2024 to Nov 2026.

Glassnode’s analysis also pointed out this trend that Bitcoin ETF trading volume has fallen below its median since launch, indicating reduced market participation and interest.

Combined with sustained outflows, this alluded to weakening investor demand. However, lower volume can also reflect reduced speculative activity rather than outright bearish sentiment.

However, at times, periods of low interest and reduced market participation can offer better accumulation opportunities than periods of excessive excitement.

Here, it’s worth pointing out that these netflows coincided with BTC’s price trading close to $61,330 at press time, after a drop of 24% over the past month.

At the same time, Bitcoin has recorded several negative netflow readings throughout November, with the latest reading at around -1.1K BTC. In other words, more Bitcoin may be leaving exchanges than entering them. While this can reduce potential selling pressure on exchanges, it does not necessarily confirm accumulation.

Meanwhile, the Bitcoin Spot Volume Bubble Map revealed a noticeable change in market activity, with trading activity not particularly overheated during the price decline.

However, with recent readings showing some heating signs as the price approached the $60,000-level, caution will be useful.

#5012. Bitcoin’s supply squeeze narrative faces scrutiny after 11,000 BTC moves to Kraken

Coinbase and Binance sent large amounts of Bitcoin to Kraken during the downturn, even as combined exchange reserves declined.

More than 11,000 BTC moved from Coinbase and Binance to Kraken during Saturday's selloff, complicating Bitcoin's supply-squeeze narrative.

The five transactions, identified by Ask The Tape (ATT), came as major exchanges recorded hundreds of millions of dollars in net Bitcoin withdrawals, fueling expectations that fewer coins were available for trading.

However, the transfers revealed substantial movements between trading venues, with Bitcoin leaving some exchanges only to replenish reserves elsewhere.

Coinbase accounted for approximately 4,028 BTC across two transactions. The first delivered 2,177.79 BTC to Kraken-tagged addresses at 03:47 UTC, followed by another 1,850 BTC at 10:04 UTC.

Binance then transferred 7,000 BTC through three transactions: two transfers of 2,000 BTC each and a third involving 3,000 BTC.

Nov. 21, 2026 UTC. The bands follow BTC from wallets tagged to Coinbase and Binance into Kraken-tagged wallets.

Withdrawals at the sending venues became gross receipts at another tracked exchange.

The five transactions are a sample of Nov. 21 UTC activity. ATT supplies wallet-cluster labels. Beneficial ownership, trades and full-day net flows remain outside the diagram’s scope.

The movements coincided with substantial differences in exchange flows reported by CryptoQuant.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Binance recorded $680 million in net Bitcoin withdrawals on Nov. 21, while Coinbase and Bybit registered $373 million and $103 million, respectively. Together, the three exchanges lost approximately $1.16 billion.

Kraken moved in the opposite direction, receiving $478 million in net inflows and offsetting 41% of those withdrawals.

That left the four platforms with a combined net outflow of $678 million, even as substantial amounts of Bitcoin circulated between their wallets.

Kraken also recorded significant outgoing transactions that day, including one involving approximately 4,028 BTC, illustrating the rapid movement of liquidity across exchanges during the downturn.

The exchange movements have become particularly significant as Bitcoin struggles to recover from its recent decline below $60,000.

Although declining exchange reserves can reduce the amount of Bitcoin immediately available for sale, sustained buying demand remains essential to turning that scarcity into upward price pressure.

That demand has weakened considerably among US institutional investors.

According to Farside Investors, US spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $1.7 billion in net withdrawals last week, extending a period of heavy redemptions that has stripped billions of dollars from the funds this month.

While Bitcoin subsequently recovered above $61,000 on Nov. 23, the ETF withdrawals have yet to show a corresponding recovery in institutional appetite.

The next test arrives Wednesday, when the US Bureau of Economic Analysis publishes October's personal consumption expenditures inflation report alongside its second estimate of third-quarter economic growth.

Both releases are scheduled for 8:30 a.m. ET and could influence expectations for the Federal Reserve's December interest rate decision.

Stronger inflation could push Treasury yields and the dollar higher, adding pressure to Bitcoin at a time when ETF investors are withdrawing capital.

That leaves Bitcoin's recovery dependent on two developments: exchange reserves continuing to decline across trading venues and fresh buying demand returning to absorb available supply.

If ETF withdrawals persist while Bitcoin continues circulating between exchanges, the apparent supply squeeze may offer little support against another wave of selling.

#5011. US Strikes Back After $6 Billion Binance Hack

The United States Department of Justice (DOJ) has initiated a significant legal push to address the theft of over $6 billion worth of cryptocurrency from Binance by North Korean hackers last month.

As per a Nov. 23 announcement, the DOJ said that it has filed a civil forfeiture complaint to recover over $1.1 billion worth of stolen cryptocurrency as part of its efforts to retrieve the billions lost in the massive hack.

The recent development comes less than a month after North Korean hackers reportedly broke into Binance on Oct. 29 in one of the biggest crypto heists ever.

The DOJ claimed that its recent civil forfeiture action focuses on over $1.1 billion worth of USD Coin (USDC), a stablecoin pegged 1:1 to the U.S. dollar and issued by Circle.

The assets are alleged to have been stolen from Binance and laundered by North Korean cybercriminals.

Significantly, the assets had previously been frozen under a restraining order obtained by the government earlier this month. With the fresh court filing, the potential recovery of the assets has taken another step forward.

The DOJ further claimed that U.S. authorities have successfully helped freeze, seize or return over $2.9 billion worth of cryptocurrency since the attack in October.

This is nearly half of the total $6 billion-plus stolen from Binance, though the government did not provide details about how much of that has been returned to the exchange.

The importance of collaboration between law enforcement agencies and crypto firms to ensure that hackers don't get away with stolen assets was stressed by Assistant Attorney General A. Tysen Duva.

“North Korean cyber hackers stole over $6 billion of cryptocurrency from Binance. But prompt coordination with both the government and our private sector partners helped to avoid the bulk of these assets from being lost,” Duva said.

U.S. authorities have further revealed the sophisticated money laundering techniques used by North Korean hackers to conceal their stolen cryptocurrency.

FBI Cyber Branch Operations Director Brett Leatherman said that the regime uses intricate money laundering methods to convert stolen digital assets into funds for its illicit activities.

After the Binance hack, authorities quickly worked to trace the stolen funds and coordinate with crypto service providers to block further transfers.

The U.S. Secret Service claimed that its investigators were able to detect and freeze some of the stolen funds within minutes of the hack.

"The bad actors in this intrusion thought they could run from law enforcement. They've found out they were wrong," said Secret Service Special Agent in Charge Todd Bratz.

#5010. Nvidia, AMD, Micron Sink as Anthropic Closes 17% Above IPO Price

Nvidia, AMD, Micron Sink as Anthropic Closes 17% Above IPO Price

Anthropic made stock market history Thursday, raising $81.34 billion in the largest initial IPO share sale ever. But Wall Street's response was considerably less enthusiastic than the demand for the Claude developer's shares.

The stock opened at $111.50 on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT, a 34% premium over its $83 offering price, briefly pushing the company's fully diluted valuation past $2 trillion.

The rally faded quickly, however, with shares dropping toward $93 before closing today at $97.17.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The offering involved 980 million shares and surpassed SpaceX's original $75 billion IPO in June. As Decrypt previously reported, SpaceX's expanded final offering reached $86.25 billion, meaning Anthropic has not surpassed that completed deal.

Investors initially welcomed the listing, sending technology stocks higher before the opening bell. By the afternoon, however, the Nasdaq Composite was down 2.06%, the S&P 500 had fallen 1.9%, and the Dow Jones Industrial Average was off 2.17%.

The selloff extended across the AI supply chain. A Thursday afternoon market heatmap showed Nvidia falling 3.4%, Broadcom losing 2.07%, and memory chipmaker Micron slipping 2.17%. Microsoft was down 1.97%, while cloud infrastructure and software provider Oracle declined 2.97%.

AMD was among the hardest hit, down 7.73% in the day, although its decline had a separate catalyst. Reuters reported that the chipmaker came under pressure after investigative outlet Hunterbrook disclosed that its affiliated investment fund had taken a short position in the company.

The picture was not uniformly negative. Palo Alto Networks rose 6%, CrowdStrike gained 2.24%, and Intel added 2.25% in the same snapshot. The divergence shows that investors were distinguishing between companies rather than selling every technology stock indiscriminately.

The broader weakness comes as investors question the cost of building AI infrastructure and the returns companies can generate from that spending. Nvidia's latest earnings beat expectations, but uncertainty following Jensen Huang's resignation has continued to weigh on the semiconductor giant.

Anthropic also faces an expensive expansion. Its IPO disclosures detailed approximately $900.9 billion in infrastructure and computing commitments extending through 2045, alongside another $150 billion to $175 billion in estimated payments under less precisely defined arrangements.

"Anthropic's record-breaking IPO has failed to rekindle enthusiasm for tech stocks," Joe Maher, market economist at Capital Economics, told Reuters.

Nvidia's weakness was notable given its latest results. The chipmaker reported $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue on Tuesday, up 103% from a year earlier, and forecast stronger-than-expected sales for the following quarter.

Even those numbers failed to erase concerns over the departure of longtime CEO Jensen Huang after allegations of extramarital affairs and possible leaks of corporate secrets and the sustainability of the AI spending boom.

The pressure also reached companies that rent out computing capacity. Neocloud operators CoreWeave and Nebius fell 5.0% and 7.8%, respectively. Software companies were weak as well: Salesforce declined 3.17% and Adobe lost 2.03% in the afternoon heatmap. From chips and data centers to enterprise software, the losses extended beyond any single corner of the AI trade.

Anthropic's IPO prospectus shows how closely these companies are tied together. Nvidia and Google each committed roughly $10 billion to a conditional private share placement alongside the IPO.

AMD agreed to invest $2.5 billion; Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft committed $1 billion apiece; and TSMC pledged $500 million. The $26 billion placement is separate from the public offering. Several of these investors are also key suppliers or infrastructure partners in the AI ecosystem.

That creates a central question for investors: How much of the money flowing into AI companies will ultimately translate into profitable demand for chips, cloud capacity and software?

Anthropic's blockbuster debut demonstrates demand for its shares, but Thursday's broader decline shows that Wall Street is not ready to extend that confidence to every company financing or supplying the AI boom.

Pearl [PEARL] has vastly outperformed the rest of the crypto market. The Layer 1 proof-of-useful-work blockchain project, aimed at utilizing artificial intelligence computation, has rallied 20.47% in 24 hours and is up 46% over the past week.

Co-founder of thesis-driven crypto hedge fund Syncracy Capital, Ryan Watkins, wrote that Pearl could address some shortcomings of the preeminent store of value in crypto, Bitcoin [BTC].

The potential of quantum computing to break Bitcoin (and most of the rest of the financial landscape), as well as Bitcoin competing with AI for the same data center infrastructure, were two hurdles that Bitcoin has no answer to, Watkins believed.

At its core, the L1 network allows a way for data center infrastructure to simultaneously handle AI inference workloads while also working to secure a network.

Arbitrary matrix multiplication could be transformed into useful proof of work, research showed. Being able to address one of the “biggest structural flaws” of Bitcoin was central to Pearl’s value proposition.

Only time will tell whether the market is swayed by this thesis.

The decline in spot trading volume is also an important sign: spot investors dropped around 22.5% of their trading activities throughout the month.

The drop in futures volume also makes the picture slightly more complicated than it should be. Following the price breakdown, the volatility increase was followed by an increase in open interest, so the drop we're witnessing right now is more of a return to the baseline than an issue on its own.

The current price resistance and support points are quite diverse, but not thoroughly tested, apart from a local support level at $0.9. The immediate breakout test is going to take place at approximately $0.99, with a possibility of a further recovery. The level at $1.00 is more of a psychological barrier, while $1.04 is the threshold that should mark the end of the downtrend.

According to CoinMarketCap's data, Monero has been steadily underperforming XRP and Dogecoin in the last 24 hours, though it's important to separate rising demand and active speculative buying. In the last 24 hours, Monero has gained over 4.7% in price. Futures open interest has crossed the $230 million mark.

As already mentioned, Monero is a weaker performer out of the three, with a 4.7% gain over the last 24 hours. Unexpectedly, a deeper look reveals a set of issues: spot and futures volume, open interest and liquidation volume suggest a rather stale market with no momentum rather than an explosive recovery that might turn into a proper uptrend. The good news is that most of the liquidated volume is on the short side.

From the network usage perspective, Monero is struggling, with its transaction count dropping below the 30-day average, prompting baseless price resilience. Though Monero's network design certainly limits the possibility of a thorough analysis due to its protected transaction design.

The most important development around Monero was, of course, the most recent Wrapped Monero incident. Despite the wrapped token itself having little to do with the main network of the asset, the coin itself had to withstand the rippling selling pressure. The token lost more than 99% of its value, causing $13 million in losses.

The main network itself went through a security audit on November 18, which found no high- or medium-severity vulnerabilities and only minor security issues, which developers have been notified about.

If the technical side of the question is rather important, the most important immediate price levels are the 508-510 range, which corresponds to the current breakout area, and the immediate support at $500.

Dogecoin's technical recovery attempt is hitting somewhat of a wall, with daily, weekly and four-hour trends showing a clear bearish dynamic in addition to the price moving below all 8 tracked moving averages on the chart.

As the CoinGlass data suggests, the trading activity we are witnessing today is mixed: spot and futures volumes are descending, with a slight increase in open interest. $584 million in futures volume and $86 million in spot volume. Long and short liquidations are balanced.

Unfortunately, the lack of network activity suggests a baseless price recovery on the market right now: only 37 thousand addresses were active in the last 24 hours, a 24% decrease YTD. Additionally, explorer data suggests that the ten largest DOGE addresses hold more than 44% of the token's available supply.

It doesn't suggest that only 10 users hold a large part of the token's supply, but rather a high concentration of DOGE on exchanges, which quite often prompts additional selling pressure.

With the low volume and open interest, any technical price level, both resistance and support, can get invalidated swiftly; however, highlighting them could be useful. The first resistance rests at $0.0599, with a trend recovery threshold located around $0.06.

Support levels lie all the way down to the important psychological level of $0.05, the nearest being $0.054. The obvious trend reversal threshold is, of course, $0.06. Until the price establishes a foothold above it, discussing a recovery is not the best option out there.

#5007. GPT-6 Astra and Opus 5.5 expose a blind spot in AI oversight

In a METR exercise, Opus 5.5 scored GPT-6 Astra’s proposed AI tests about 0.2 points higher on a 0–1 scale than a human scorer did. A gap that large could affect which tests get pursued.

In METR’s test of AI oversight, Opus 5.5 gave GPT-6 Astra’s proposed AI tests higher scores than the human evaluator did. METR said the gap could change whether its scoring rules recommended pursuing a test. In a separate exercise, leading AI agents placed ahead of most human entrants across 15 past programming contests.

Across Al Zimmermann’s programming contests, METR reported median placements from the 96th to the 98th percentile for Fable 5.1, Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra. About four people still beat the strongest models in the median contest.

The agents had 48 hours per contest on a 28-core machine. Each submission gave them a numerical score they could use to see whether their changes improved the result. Human contestants typically had about three calendar months, used their own hardware and could see the public standings. METR compared the agents’ scores with the original contest results.

In a separate exercise, METR asked agents to design tests that would help researchers assess what AI models could do. AI judges and a human scorer had access to project files, scores from trial runs and the agents’ case for why their tests were relevant and feasible. The human scorer assessed whether each proposed test was worth including in METR’s suite. METR said none of the designs was useful enough to include in its report.

The AI judges tended to agree more closely with the human scorer when a weaker model had designed the test. When the model that designed the test was as capable as the judge or stronger, the judges tended to rate the work more highly than the human did. Opus 5.5 agreed most closely with the human scorer, yet rated GPT-6 Astra’s proposals about 0.2 points higher on average on a scale from zero to one.

The comparison came from one exercise, and what counted as useful depended in part on METR’s research priorities. METR said models underused some resources in the judgment tests. It also reported strong model performance on some longer machine-learning tasks with automatic scoring. A separate AI peer review audit tested whether reviewers’ objections changed the findings by running code on the underlying data.

#5005. Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi has updated its online banking terms to make customers responsible for the actions of third-party AI agents accessing their accounts, including when an agent leaks their credentials and someone else uses them to get in.

The change, reported by TechCrunch Wednesday, makes explicit how the bank intends to handle an increasingly awkward question: Who pays when software handling your money makes a mistake?

Under Citi’s new terms, actions taken using credentials supplied to an outside agent are treated as authorized by the customer, even if the tool ignores instructions, exceeds its permissions, or shares those credentials with another party.

Citi says it has no obligation to investigate whether the agent followed the customer's original prompts, subject to applicable law.

A Citi spokesperson told TechCrunch that the November revision clarifies the risks of sharing banking credentials or granting outside tools account access. The provision does not apply to AI agents offered by Citi itself.

Citi is not acting alone. JPMorgan and Bank of America announced similar policies in October, while Citizens Bank warned customers this week by email that they could be responsible for what their AI assistants do.

PNC also changed its digital agreement on Wednesday. Its rules distinguish bank-approved connections, which can provide limited access, from handing an agent unrestricted access to an account.

The move follows a banking security scare from October 22. Major banks had detected AI agents logging into customer accounts, sometimes taking steps that appeared to go beyond their users' instructions, and executives said they struggled to determine which agents had been properly authorized.

An AI agent differs from a conventional chatbot because it can act on a user's behalf, potentially navigating websites, accessing financial records, or initiating payments. That makes a mistaken instruction or compromised login more consequential than a bad answer about a bank balance.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency has since issued AI examiner guidance that addresses outside agents, payment controls, and human approval. The framework is nonbinding and does not itself create new requirements for banks.

Citi's language does not automatically eliminate consumer fraud protections. Under U.S. federal consumer protection rules, banks generally must investigate unauthorized electronic transfers and limit customers' financial liability for fraud. Those protections cannot simply be waived through a banking agreement, although their application to transactions performed by customer-authorized AI agents remains less clear.

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau says banks cannot use agreements to waive those legal protections, although the rules distinguish theft by an outsider from transfers actually authorized by a customer or someone given access.

Whether a particular AI-related loss qualifies as unauthorized depends on how the transaction occurred.

PNC's updated agreement takes a stricter operational approach: Customers may use certain connection methods approved by the bank, but cannot give third-party AI agents their passwords or one-time security codes, and agents generally cannot initiate payments unless PNC expressly permits it.

#5004. Can Institutions Secretly Buy Billions of XRP? Ripple Veteran Says It Is 'Not Impossible'

Ripple’s longtime CTO emeritus David Schwartz does not think institutions can privately snap up billions of XRP via dark pools without affecting the token’s price on public markets.

"I don't think so. Who are they buying the XRP from? Why wouldn't people generally want the price to rise when they buy? But it's not impossible," Schwartz wrote on X on Nov. 24.

This was in response to a question posed by an XRP community member wondering if private trading platforms might give institutions an advantage over retail investors.

Earlier in the conversation, Schwartz noted the pros and cons of dark pools for retail traders.

He mentioned one disadvantage: less efficient price discovery. Given that bids and offers are obscured, retail traders in public markets have less insight into other traders’ bids and ask prices.

If there is a large XRP holder who is already inclined to sell at the current price, an institution could arrange a private trade with this holder without needing to place a massive buy order on an exchange. They could execute the trade without driving the public price up as they buy.

However, will there be enough sellers interested in offloading billions of XRP like this? One might run out of sellers willing to sell at that price. So, the buyer might need to up the ante to find the next willing seller.

A dark pool can hide order size, but one still needs someone on the other side to execute that order.

Plus, a buyer will naturally prefer not to tip its hand while accumulating a position.

Schwartz left open the possibility of a private purchase at scale, but the possibility is rather low.

When asked why XRP Ledger developments were not doing anything for XRP’s price on Nov. 9, Schwartz pointed to the broader crypto market.

He also debunked the notion that Ripple had some secret plan it was not sharing with holders. The company regularly talks about its plans.

Schwartz also mentioned a practical benefit of XRP being worth more in the: a higher price could mean cheaper liquidity provision and less slippage (the difference between the expected price a trader pays and the price they actually pay).

At the same time, he was less convinced that demand for XRP to pay transaction fees could have a big impact on the price by itself.

#5003. CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

The UK's main crypto trade association has called on the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) to make sure that developers and governance participants in decentralized finance protocols are not treated as regulated firms by default.

CryptoUK set out its position in a response to the FCA's consultation paper CP26/36, which proposes guidance on the "perimeter" of the UK's new crypto regime: which activities fall inside it and need FCA authorization, and which don't. The group, which says it represents more than 150 crypto companies, submitted its response on Wednesday, November 18, the day the consultation closed, and published it a day later.

Yesterday, CryptoUK submitted its response to the Financial Conduct Authority’s Consultation Paper CP26/36, which proposes amendments to the Perimeter Guidance Manual (PERG) to reflect the FSMA 2000 (Cryptoassets) (Miscellaneous Amendments) Regulations 2026.

The consultation… pic.twitter.com/oOrZgAWscP

— CryptoUK (@CryptoUKAssoc) November 19, 2026

"Software development, maintenance, security work and participation in decentralised governance should not, by themselves, establish that a person carries on a regulated activity," CryptoUK wrote. That would be consistent with the FCA's "clear controlling person" approach to DeFi, under which regulation should hinge on who effectively controls a financial service, it argued.

CryptoUK said it "broadly" supports the draft guidance, which reflects further amendments to the UK's crypto legislation that the government laid in September. The FCA aims to publish its final guidance in January 2027. It welcomed new exclusions from the regime for temporary settlement and payment arrangements, assets backing UK-recognized stablecoins, proprietary trading and market making, and technical service providers.

But in several places, the draft "goes further than the legislation," the group said. It pointed to a proposed link to the UK based on where a protocol is developed or governed, requirements in the exclusions for payments and proprietary trading, and indicators that would narrow the exclusion for technical services. The FCA has acknowledged that the timing of the latest amendments created uncertainty for some firms, and said it will give applicants extra time where questions over the technical services exclusion held up their applications for authorization.

The group asked the FCA to publish a public record of stablecoins that qualify under the UK regime, to clarify how stablecoins differ from electronic money, and to include more examples of activities that typically fall outside the perimeter.

CryptoUK also urged the FCA to raise with the Treasury outcomes that put UK firms at a disadvantage, citing the treatment of UK technical providers that offer access to regulated overseas trading venues, and of payments using stablecoins issued abroad. The UK should align with the EU's MiCA framework "where appropriate," it said. The response was prepared with law firm CMS and signed by CryptoUK policy director Nick Brooks.

The consultation is part of the FCA's work to build out the UK's crypto rulebook. The regulator finalized its core rules for crypto firms in June, ahead of a regime due to take effect on October 25, 2027, and later issued guidance two weeks before its authorization window for crypto firms opened. It has also set out a shared vision for tokenization with the Bank of England.

#5002. RLUSD supply grew 30% on Ethereum and fell 20% on XRPL – Assessing…

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

RLUSD is growing while USDT and USDC are losing supply.

At the macro level, the stablecoin sector is in a relatively weak phase. In Q4 alone, the market lost over $20 billion of value. As a result, the overall stablecoin market cap has fallen below $300 billion.

This means that all of the market’s growth since Q3 2025 (when it peaked at above $320 billion) has been erased. Notably, USDT and USDC account for more than half of the total liquidity outflows during this period.

In contrast, RLUSD has increased by almost 20% during this period. This represents an increase of almost $500 million in total supply, pushing its total market cap to a record $2.9 billion.

In essence, RLUSD is attracting fresh liquidity even as the broader stablecoin market continues to contract.

This raises an important question: Which L1 stands to benefit the most from this growth?

Fundamentally, Ethereum supports much larger amounts of RLUSD in comparison to XRPL, with 72%+ of RLUSD ($2.08 billion) on Ethereum. In addition, supply growth on Ethereum is relatively higher.

In the last 30 days, RLUSD supply has increased by 30% on Ethereum, whereas supply has fallen by 20% on XRPL.

So, with RLUSD liquidity increasingly concentrated on Ethereum even as the broader stablecoin market faces outflows, Ethereum seems to be the clear winner. However, supply is only one side of the story, and XRPL’s transaction volume shows why it should not be overlooked.

Growing stablecoin supply and actually using that supply are two different things.

Interestingly, this difference is becoming important in comparing Ethereum [ETH] and XRPL’s on-chain activity around RLUSD. On-chain data shows that nearly $11.716 billion worth of RLUSD was transacted on the XRP Ledger and Ethereum in the week up to the 1st of November 2026.

The key takeaway? XRPL handled the larger share of RLUSD activity. As the chart below shows, $6.699 billion was transacted on the XRPL network, representing 57% of the total RLUSD volume. The Ethereum blockchain had total transactions of $5.017 billion (42.82%).

So, although there is more RLUSD on the Ethereum blockchain, XRPL is seeing higher transaction volume.

Notably, RLUSD’s DEX activity on XRPL is also picking up.

The total DEX volume reached an all-time high of $596.2 million in the week ending August. While volume has dropped in recent weeks, it has been consistently above $70 million per week for the last 10 weeks.

More recently, volume has picked up again, exceeding $200 million per week. This is significantly higher than previous averages of approximately $15 million.

So, while there is more RLUSD on Ethereum, XRPL is seeing higher transaction volume and growing DEX activity. This suggests that more RLUSD is being used on XRPL, making it a network to watch as the stablecoin continues to grow.

RLUSD supply is growing despite broader stablecoin outflows, with Ethereum holding over 72% of its total supply.

XRPL handles 57% of RLUSD transaction volume and is seeing a sharp rise in DEX activity.

#5001. Inkling 2 Mini got 20 questions right but failed 4 out of 5 tests that required deeper thinking

Inkling 2 Mini’s repeated queries on a hidden-pattern test spotlight the need for tighter limits as local AI agents reach Macs.

In a hosted test published by Plotly on Nov. 21, the AI model Inkling 2 Mini got all 20 routine data questions right.

On one question about a hidden pattern, it made 585 queries over about 48 minutes and repeated the same few queries dozens of times, but it still missed the pattern.

In Plotly’s benchmark, Inkling 2 Mini scored 36 of 43 overall and found one of five hidden patterns. Plotly found that on the hidden-pattern questions, the model often added up the biggest totals and stopped.

Plotly ran the model through its own analytics software, Plotly Studio Embedded, using a version hosted by Prime Intellect and accessed through OpenRouter. It reported one scored sample per model, combining results with reruns of 16 Inkling 2 Mini questions that encountered a provider outage.

One scored sample using hosted inference.

On hidden-pattern questions, the model often stopped at overall totals before isolating the local signal.

The test leaves open how often the query loop would recur.

Thinking Machines Lab released the downloadable weights on Nov. 20 under the Apache 2.0 license. Its model card lists support for Apple silicon Macs with at least 32 GB of memory.

The card lists a context window of up to 1 million tokens, which measures how much information the model can handle at once. How much of that window is usable on a particular Mac depends on the memory needed to process the request.

Once a request fits, a separate limit can stop an agent from spending more turns on the same few queries.

Thinking Machines Lab says it saw the model repeatedly check its work without making progress in internal tests. The lab recommends caps on generated tokens and on the number of turns an agent can take.

#5000. Ethereum Foundation Sounds Alarm Over AI Threats

The Ethereum Foundation has been working for years to secure the network against a future in which quantum computing breaks the cryptography underpinning Ethereum, but it is now facing a new and more immediate concern around artificial intelligence.

AI is identifying bugs in live software more quickly than human reviewers can keep up with, and the Ethereum Foundation has now doubled the size of its protocol security team, according to a recent announcement.

As Kev Wedderburn wrote in a post from Devcon, AI models are now identifying thousands of bugs in the Linux kernel, one of the most scrutinized codebases in the world.

For Ethereum developers, the immediate challenge is ensuring that bugs are not present in their software before an upgrade.

The longer-term challenge is mathematical. Ethereum uses digital signatures to identify who can spend funds and which validators have approved the state of the chain.

Wedderburn says that AI's advances in analyzing decades-old algorithms raise questions about the mathematical assumptions underlying those signatures that researchers should consider.

It is not clear whether AI could break Ethereum's existing cryptography. However,  the Ethereum Foundation wants work on new cryptography to be underway.

After more than a year of considering various signature schemes, the Ethereum Foundation has decided to replace elliptic-curve signatures with hash-based cryptography.

The argument is that Ethereum already relies on cryptographic hash security, and by using hashes for signatures too, it can remove an additional assumption about the difficulty of solving mathematical problems associated with elliptic curves.

The proposal affects three areas of Ethereum.

It would see validators use hash-based keys for their signatures, which in turn changes how block finality is determined on the network.

Wallet developers would need to provide users with a way to transition their accounts to hash-based signatures.

And Ethereum's change to the cryptography used for commitments to data (blobs) that rollups publish to the network would require adjustments from rollups too.

One challenge is the size of the signatures. Hash-based signatures are much larger, so developers need to consider how that affects the hardware needed to run a node at home.

Staking pools and multisig wallets would need adjustments too. So would smart contracts that rely on existing cryptography.

There will also be a need to adjust tools that rely on the signature scheme currently used by Ethereum validators (BLS).
