#4941. SBF’s 'The Altruists' Plot Twist: FTX’s Anthropic Shares Would Be Worth $43 Billion

SBF’s 'The Altruists' Plot Twist: FTX’s Anthropic Shares Would Be Worth $43 Billion

Sam Bankman-Fried had an eventful Thursday—at least on the internet.

Anthropic went public, Netflix released “The Altruists,” and Crypto Twitter imagined the fortune his collapsed exchange might have made by keeping its AI investment.

At Anthropic’s November 19 closing price of $97.17, FTX’s former shares would have been worth $43.28 billion. At the $111.50 opening trade, their theoretical value would have reached $49.66 billion.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That money never belonged personally to Bankman-Fried. The bankruptcy estate sold the shares to repay creditors after FTX’s 2022 collapse.

The former CEO is serving a 25-year sentence for fraud after using FTX customer funds to make investments and cover losses, plus got hit with $11 billion in forfeiture—a fraction of what his single Anthropic investment would have returned today.

A company linked to Alameda, FTX’s sister company, committed $500 million to Anthropic in October 2021. The money converted into roughly 44.54 million preferred shares during the AI startup’s 2022 Series B, about 13.6% of the company.

Bankruptcy administrators sold the position in two batches in 2024 for approximately $1.34 billion. Buyers included an Abu Dhabi-linked fund and Jane Street, Bankman-Fried’s former employer, as filings showed.

At Thursday’s closing price that investment would have produced a total of nearly $43.28 billion—roughly 32 times what creditors’ administrators actually collected.

Sadly, that fortune is hypothetical. Later financing diluted FTX’s percentage ownership, the shares carried restrictions, and the estate needed liquidity to compensate customers who lost money when FTX went belly up. A judge approved the sale in February 2024.

Tiffany Fong, who rose to Twitter fame after interviewing SBF just before he was arrested, shared an AI-generated video of a crying Bankman-Fried drinking in a car alongside the $40 billion-plus calculation. Another account posted a crying-man meme about him watching the listing from prison.

That’s good for a 1,018% return, and proof SBF knew how to pick his bets. But even the best investments can't justify gambling with other people's money without their consent. He made that bet, too—and lost.

#4938. CFTC prediction market rules advance to White House review amid state-federal jurisdiction fight

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

In June, the CFTC proposed new rules to delineate its jurisdiction over certain bets made on prediction markets, as those platforms have grown on a massive billion-dollar scale.

A proposed set of rules by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission to further assert its oversight of prediction markets is now with the White House for review.

The agency filed "Prediction Markets; Public Interest Determinations" with the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs (OIRA), a division within the Office of Management and Budget that reviews federal regulations before publication.

"Staff is considering recommending that the Commission propose rules relating to prediction markets to help clarify the regulatory framework for event contracts and provide greater certainty to market participants," according to the filing on OIRA's website.

In June, the CFTC proposed new rules to delineate its jurisdiction over certain bets made on prediction markets, as those platforms have grown on a massive billion-dollar scale. The agency's proposed rules would restrict event contracts tied to gaming, war, terrorism, and assassination. Over 1,300 comments have been filed on the proposed rule.

When it comes to a controversial spot between states and the federal government — sports contracts — the CFTC says it's their game despite pushback from state regulators.

Under Chair Michael Selig, the CFTC has maintained that it holds "exclusive jurisdiction" over event contracts. Through proposed rulemaking, the agency has argued that prediction market contracts constitute swaps because they are tied to future events, placing them under federal regulation rather than state laws.

States, however, contend that prediction market platforms are effectively operating illegal gambling businesses while avoiding state regulatory requirements and tax obligations. The dispute has escalated into a series of lawsuits involving nearly a dozen states and the CFTC, with courts reaching conflicting conclusions.

Citing those conflicting rulings, former CFTC Commissioner Christy Goldsmith Romero said last Thursday that the case could reach the Supreme Court.

"I think it's likely that the prediction markets may end up losing on the sports contracts," she said at the Capitol Crypto Forum.

On Friday, a coalition of 44 attorneys general said they opposed the CFTC's prediction market rulemaking. New Jersey has asked the U.S. Supreme Court to hear the case and decide whether the federal government or states have jurisdiction.

#4935. Strive (ASST) Buys No Bitcoin Last Week, Holdings Stay at 43,404 BTC

Strive (Nasdaq: ASST) bought no bitcoin last week and sold no stock to fund new purchases, leaving its holdings unchanged at 43,404 BTC, according to a Monday 8-K filing.

The Dallas-based company reported that its bitcoin count was unchanged between Nov. 13 and Nov. 20. It sold no Class A common shares, no SATA preferred shares and no SATS preferred shares in its at-the-market programs from the Nov. 13 close through the Nov. 20 close.

The pause follows a slowdown from the October sprint that built the stack. Strive bought 251 bitcoin for $17.5 million the prior week at an average of $69,853 per coin, after adding just 29 coins the week before that.

Bitcoin traded near $61,000 on Coinbase on Monday afternoon. That is about 12.6% below the $69,853 average Strive paid for its most recent purchase. At that price, the 43,404-coin stack is worth roughly $2.65 billion.

The filing gives no reason for the pause, and the company has not said whether it plans to resume weekly buying. Strive has typically disclosed its holdings on Mondays, so next week’s update will show whether the stack grows again.

Most of the stack was built quickly. Strive held 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 and acquired 15,124 more between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30, including a single 6,182-coin purchase. The company closed a registered offering of 7 million SATS shares on Oct. 20 for about $609 million in net proceeds, which funded much of that run.

Strive’s cash and cash equivalents slipped to $462.4 million as of Nov. 20 from $465.3 million a week earlier, a decline of $2.9 million. The filing does not break out what drove the change.

The company also still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred stock. The fair value of that position fell to $47.3 million from $50.5 million, a drop of about $3.2 million, with the share count unchanged.

Share counts were also flat. Strive reported 118,133,002 Class A shares and 9,198,107 Class B shares, for 127,331,109 effective common shares. Outstanding preferred stood at 14,030,176 shares of SATA and 7,141,037 shares of SATS, the 15% perpetual preferred it has used to fund most of its purchases.

Strive’s holdings rose from 43,153 bitcoin on Nov. 6 to 43,404 on Nov. 13, and the filing shows no change since. With no common or preferred stock sold, the company raised no new capital through its offering programs last week, which left cash as its main resource for dividends and operations.

Strive shares were trading near $15.55 at 3:56 p.m. ET on Monday, up about 7.1% from Friday’s close of $14.51, according to Yahoo Finance. The stock opened near $17.64, a gain of more than 20%, and gave back much of it through the session.

The swing tracked bitcoin-linked stocks broadly after Nvidia reinstated CEO Jensen Huang and sent equities sharply higher, a move Bitcoin Magazine covered earlier Monday. Bitcoin spiked above $64,000 in the morning before sliding back toward $61,000 during U.S. trading hours.

The stock has lost much of its October run. ASST closed at $30.75 on Oct. 21, so Monday’s price is about 49% lower. It is still roughly 5% above its 2025 year-end close of $14.76.

Strive’s preferred stocks remain a central part of its model. On Nov. 12, the board raised the SATA dividend rate to 13.25% for periods beginning Dec. 1, while SATS continues to pay 15%. Both pay daily cash dividends, so the company’s cash balance funds a steady outflow even in weeks without purchases.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $61,000.

Investors have also grown wary of circular AI financing deals, in which chipmakers help customers fund chip purchases and data center construction. On Tuesday’s earnings call, interim CEO Colette Kress said Nvidia would be far more selective about such financing going forward.

The crisis has erased more than a third of Nvidia’s market value, even after the company beat expectations Tuesday with $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue, up 103% from a year earlier.

The amount comes from the balances listed in the complaint, filed Monday in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia. U.S. investigators attributed the theft to North Korean military hackers identified in the complaint as Lazarus Group/Advanced Persistent Threat 38 (APT38).

Circle has blocked access to the USDC but told prosecutors it cannot transfer the original tokens from the blocked addresses. The complaint says Circle "has agreed to voluntarily issue new USDC in an amount equivalent to the amount of the Defendant Property" and transfer it to the United States "upon receipt of an Order of Forfeiture from the Court."

In its Nov. 12 announcement, the DOJ said it had obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion tied to the hack. The DOJ said Monday that U.S. authorities have helped freeze, seize or return more than $2.9 billion in crypto since the theft.

"Following the Binance theft, the FBI and our partners moved quickly to trace and freeze stolen assets and share critical information with virtual asset service providers," said Brett Leatherman, operations director of the FBI's Cyber Branch.

Binance put the losses from the hack at about $6.34 billion and said it fully replenished the affected assets at its own expense. The exchange separately obtained a $6.5 billion worldwide freezing order from an Abu Dhabi court as part of its effort to recover the stolen funds.

#4925. Claude Claims Historic Math Breakthrough One Day After Anthropic IPO

Claude Claims Historic Math Breakthrough One Day After Anthropic IPO

An Anthropic mathematician says Claude helped him crack the Riemann hypothesis, a 167-year-old puzzle with a $1 million prize attached.

His claim surfaced Friday, one day after the AI company’s stock-market debut, and has not yet been independently verified.

“I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis,” Levent Alpöge wrote in a four-page note. He says the method also resolves the broader Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, which extends the question to other mathematical functions.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Alpöge says he and Claude encoded their argument in Lean, software used to check mathematical proofs step by step. His note links to 14 downloadable parts of the proof archive, but its availability does not amount to acceptance by the mathematical community.

Alpöge says he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei about a possible announcement “late Sunday night,” November 15. He then consulted company leadership and said there was a constraint to respect, without explaining precisely what it was.

“The precise timing is horrible,” he acknowledged. Alpöge rejected a cynical interpretation of the timing, but did not explicitly say the company’s IPO caused the delay.

Anthropic began trading on Nasdaq Thursday, November 19, after raising $81.34 billion in its offering.

Alpöge figured in September’s AI math wars. OpenAI claimed it had solved the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem as he and NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster pursued related fluid-equation work, sparking a credit dispute over research priority.

OpenAI followed in October with 722 manuscripts generated by an unreleased model, grouped into 372 families of results. Only 162 included Lean-checked main results, and mathematicians demanded stronger evidence rather than accepting the company’s flood of claims at face value.

Some mathematicians fear a “mathocalypse,” with AI solving coveted problems before researchers can learn from pursuing them. Toronto mathematician Daniel Litt warned that, without adaptation, “there’s just no more math in 50 years.” At an MIT panel this week, Buckmaster argued mathematics still requires human understanding.

First posed by Bernhard Riemann in 1859, the hypothesis predicts where certain zeros of the Riemann zeta function fall. The question matters because those zeros are linked to the distribution of prime numbers, whose patterns underlie extensive mathematical research.

A proof would be a historic mathematical result, since experts have been trying to solve that for over a century.

Lean can verify that formal deductions obey its rules. Mathematicians still need to reproduce the archive, check the formalized theorem matches the original conjecture, and examine the claimed result before considering it settled.

Anthropic previously reported progress on the zeta function without claiming to solve the full hypothesis. Alpöge’s new assertion goes far beyond that earlier work.

The Clay Mathematics Institute lists the Riemann hypothesis among its Millennium Prize Problems, each carrying a $1 million award. Under the institute’s prize rules, a proposed solution must be published in a qualifying outlet, survive at least two years of scrutiny, and earn general acceptance before the prize can be considered.

#4921. CFTC staff say bitcoin, ether can count toward derivatives firms’ operating expenses

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

On Monday, the agency's staff released that advisory and said ether, bitcoin and payment stablecoins could be counted toward that requirement — and would be subject to a haircut.

Staff at the Commodity Futures Trading Commission issued an advisory saying certain regulated clearinghouses and exchanges may count cryptocurrencies such as bitcoin and ether toward a requirement to maintain enough assets to cover at least one year of operating expenses.

On Monday, the agency's Division of Market Oversight and Division of Clearing and Risk released that advisory and said ether, bitcoin, and payment stablecoins could be counted toward that requirement — and would be subject to a haircut — a regulatory discount applied to their market value to account for potential losses.

"Pleased to see @CFTC staff continue to provide important clarity around crypto assets in US derivatives markets through today’s staff advisory," said CFTC Chair Michael Selig in a post on X.

The advisory applies to swap execution facilities, clearing organizations and designated contract markets. It also does not create new legal obligations and is not enforceable. The CFTC also noted that crypto do not qualify as cash or very liquid securities under the Generally Accepted Accounting Principles — the set of accounting rules used by U.S. companies to report financial statements — so they are not eligible towards a liquidity requirement, which is separate.

Katherine Kirkpatrick Bos, Chainlink Labs' head of legal, called the advisory "significant."

"People don't realize that one of the impediments to these licenses is the cost of holding a lot of $ - this will help," she said in a post on X.

Also on Monday, the CFTC published its final rule creating a reporting framework for certain event contracts. Rulemaking was necessary, the CFTC said, because the agency had previously moved through a no-action letter process and those don't carry the "force of law," and it also wanted to standardize reported data.

The final rule adds a new part regarding clarifying dates for compliance.

“The CFTC will no longer regulate market participants through a patchwork of no-action letters, which serve as band-aids for unworkable regulations," Selig said in a statement. "This final rule is an important step in future-proofing the regulatory framework for event contracts."

#4919. Is the AI Bubble Finally Popping? Nvidia Crashes 48% as Wall Street Experts Sound the Alarm

Is the AI Bubble Finally Popping? Nvidia Crashes 48% as Wall Street Experts Sound the Alarm

Wall Street's artificial intelligence boom is facing its biggest test yet.

Nvidia plunged 14% on Friday, the Nasdaq Composite lost 5.3%, and the broader S&P 500 entered correction territory as investors questioned whether the trillions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will ever produce the expected returns.

The Nasdaq closed at 22,617.73, approximately 18% below its October 6 record closing high of 27,599.79. Its 10% weekly decline was the worst since 2020, while the index has now erased its gains for 2026.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Nvidia, the company that powered much of the AI rally, finished Friday at $125.53, down 47.5% from its October closing record of $239.24.

The chipmaker lost nearly 23% during the week as the fallout from former CEO Jensen Huang's departure and reports of an SEC accounting investigation added to concerns about the AI business model.

Other AI-linked companies have suffered even steeper losses. CoreWeave, which rents computing capacity to AI developers, closed at $39.51, almost 80% below its 2025 peak, while Oracle has lost roughly 70% from its highs and finished the week at $101.19.

AMD shed 20.5% over the week, closing at $424.39, while Alphabet lost 10.3% to $265.29. The iShares Semiconductor ETF, SOXX, dropped 17.4% during the same period, showing that investors are selling across the companies supplying the AI industry, not merely its most speculative businesses.

The selloff accelerated just as Anthropic completed its historic $81 billion IPO. The Claude developer priced its shares at $83 and closed its first trading session 17% higher, but its stock fell another 9% on Friday as enthusiasm for the offering failed to restore confidence in the wider sector.

With so many stocks in the red, it’s logical that many AI fanboys are starting to get nervous.

Traders on Polymarket have become increasingly pessimistic. Contracts assessing whether the AI industry will experience a defined downturn showed approximately 54% odds by December 31, 2026, and 65% by June 30, 2027, according to Friday's market snapshot.

The market has attracted approximately $4.1 million in trading volume, but its definition of a bubble bursting is more demanding than a falling stock market. At least three specific events must occur within a single 90-day period, including Nvidia closing 50% below its all-time high, SOXX falling 40%, major AI suppliers experiencing similar collapses, or severe financial problems at AI developers.

SOXX has already fallen approximately 43% from its split-adjusted record closing price, satisfying one of the specified price thresholds on paper. Nvidia is approaching another, although crossing either threshold alone would not settle the contract.

The damage has also spread into cryptocurrencies, undermining expectations that 2026 would bring a sustained recovery. Bitcoin finished the week near $58,895, down 9.8% over the period and more than 50% below its 2025 peak.

The total cryptocurrency market capitalization fell to approximately $2.01 trillion, down from more than $4 trillion at its peak. As Decrypt reported Friday, the Crypto Fear and Greed Index dropped to 9, signaling extreme fear and its weakest reading since February.

This was supposed to be the year of the usual pre-halving moon. Instead, for the first time in the history of Bitcoin, the coin is on its way to have two consecutive red years.

Bitcoin has increasingly behaved like other speculative assets sensitive to liquidity conditions, particularly when investors expect borrowing costs to remain elevated.

The pressure is also visible in investment flows. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $582.2 million in net withdrawals on Thursday, while forced liquidations added to the selling as Bitcoin broke below the psychologically important $60,000 level.

Owen Lamont, a portfolio manager at Acadian Asset Management and former University of Chicago finance professor, believes the warning signs have become difficult to ignore. After arguing in January that AI stocks were not yet in a bubble, he told Fortune this week that he now assigns a 70% probability to the U.S. stock market already being in one.

Lamont tracks four warning signs: excessive valuations, widespread belief in a speculative boom, new stock issuance, and investors pouring money into the market. His latest assessment suggests all four are now present, although his issuance figures are heavily influenced by SpaceX's massive IPO earlier this year.

Bank of America's November fund manager survey offers another indication of deteriorating confidence. Investors reduced their technology exposure by the most since 2006, and 52% expect at least one major AI hyperscaler to announce a capital spending cut within six months, Fortune reported.

The European Central Bank has also raised concerns about how AI development is being financed. Its November review estimates that capital expenditure by major American technology companies could reach $5.5 trillion cumulatively by 2030, with growing reliance on debt as infrastructure spending outpaces internally generated cash.

The similarities to the dot-com era are difficult to dismiss: investors are assigning enormous valuations to companies based on projected demand, businesses are racing to build infrastructure ahead of proven profitability, and financial markets are increasingly dependent on a small group of technology companies.

During the original dot-com collapse, the Nasdaq lost approximately 78% between March 2000 and October 2002. The present decline is considerably smaller, but history also offers a reminder that a technology can succeed commercially while the companies financing its expansion lose enormous amounts of market value.

There are important differences, however. Many of today's largest AI beneficiaries, including Nvidia, Alphabet, and Microsoft, operate profitable businesses with substantial existing revenues, unlike numerous internet startups whose valuations depended almost entirely on future growth.

Nvidia's latest quarterly results illustrate the contradiction. The company beat earnings estimates, reported record revenue and net income, and issued stronger-than-expected revenue guidance, yet its shares continued collapsing.

Richard Saperstein, chief investment officer at Treasury Partners, described the downturn as an "AI repricing" in comments to Reuters, citing slowing data center construction and tighter funding conditions. Lawrence Gillum, chief fixed income strategist at LPL Financial, offered a less alarming interpretation, saying the decline was "not a bubble bursting" and arguing that the AI investment cycle still has room to run.

Higher interest rates complicate both arguments. Investors are increasingly expecting the Federal Reserve to raise rates again in December, increasing financing costs for data centers while reducing the appeal of stocks whose valuations depend on profits years into the future.

The balance of evidence points to more than a routine bout of nerves, but less than a confirmed systemic bust. Stocks have repriced faster than the underlying technology has changed.

What happens next depends on whether actual demand for AI can justify the data-center spending spree—and whether the companies financing it can withstand tighter credit. The real test isn't simply whether Nvidia can keep selling chips. It's whether its customers can turn all that computing power into profits.

Right now much of the AI hype is just… hype. Markets are moving more by the perceived potential of AI in the future and not so much by what AI is actually doing right now.

Crypto investors have seen this before with ICOs, the TPS wars, the metaverse, the NFT fever and more. For many of them, the distinction may offer little comfort.

Bitcoin's slump shows how quickly markets can stop distinguishing between promising technologies and overpriced bets when confidence disappears. The dot-com crash didn't kill the internet; it killed the assumption that every internet company would make its investors rich.

AI may be the next world-changing technology to prove that being right about the future is not the same as making money from it.

#4917. California court terminates lawsuit against Coinbase over TerraUSD purchases

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

A federal judge has granted Coinbase's motion to dismiss a lawsuit brought by Larry Pearl, who purchased TerraUSD on Coinbase in May 2022 shortly before the token collapsed.

A U.S. federal judge has granted Coinbase's Coinbase Global, Inc. (COIN) motion to dismiss a proposed class-action lawsuit brought by Larry Pearl, who purchased the TerraUSD (UST) stablecoin on the trading platform shortly before the token collapsed.

In a Nov. 20 order, U.S. District Judge Maxine M. Chesney granted Coinbase's motion to dismiss. Judgment was entered three days later, terminating the case.

The plaintiff's latest lawsuit, which was the fourth amended complaint, asserted claims under negligent misrepresentation and violations of California's unfair competition, false advertising, and securities regulations.

According to his original complaint in June 2022, Pearl held approximately $13,000 worth of UST on Coinbase at the time of the collapse, which wiped out over 90% of the value of his holdings.

Pearl alleged that Coinbase misrepresented UST by failing to adequately disclose that it was an algorithmic stablecoin linked to sister cryptocurrency LUNA, rather than a reserve-backed asset.

On the consumer-protection claims based on alleged omissions, the court found that Coinbase's website and linked materials had already disclosed that UST was not backed by U.S. dollars held in a bank account and that minting UST required burning LUNA. The court also stated that trading data for UST and LUNA was publicly available.

The court dismissed several claims because they exceeded the scope of the limited leave to amend granted in an earlier order. The remaining claims were dismissed on the merits.

Pearl also alleged that UST was an unregistered security and that Coinbase either sold it directly or materially aided its sale with misleading statements. The court dismissed the securities claims, finding that Pearl failed to show UST was a security under the Howey test. He did not adequately allege a common enterprise or an expectation of profit.

Terra's UST lost its peg amid large withdrawals from the high-yield Anchor Protocol. The failure of the algorithmic mechanism triggered a rapid collapse. UST fell well below its dollar peg while LUNA’s supply hyperinflated, and both tokens lost nearly all of their value within days, wiping out tens of billions of dollars in market value across the Terra ecosystem.

Following the collapse, Terraform Labs co-founder Do Kwon left South Korea. He was arrested in Montenegro in March 2023 while traveling on falsified documents and was extradited to the U.S. in late 2024. In December 2025, a federal judge in New York sentenced him to 15 years in prison after he pleaded guilty to fraud charges.

#4915. The Man With No Butt

My flat ass has haunted me since high school. So I did what any man would do and sought out some wisdom from the Internet. This is the story of my midlife butt crisis.

I REMEMBER THE first time I became aware of it. Or should I say lack of it.

I grew up in the mid '80s. A magical time. MTV actually played music videos. New Coke was a thing people cared about. And a ginger-headed German teenager named Boris Becker—Boom-Boom, to his millions of fans—became, at age 17, the youngest player ever to win Wimbledon.

While other kids my age were collecting Garbage Pail Kids cards or trying to solve Rubik’s Cubes, I was rewatching VHS recordings of Becker’s matches. I practiced his signature serve motion in front of my bedroom mirror. I begged—actually begged—my parents for the complete Boris Becker starter kit: the Ellesse tennis outfit, the Puma half-top sneakers, and the holy grail itself: the Puma Super tennis racket.

The problem was, no matter how hard I tried, no matter how much Hi-C I consumed, I could never quite achieve the same explosive spring that made Boom-Boom so spectacular. Then I noticed something about Becker’s serve—the one that earned him his nickname. Most players bent their knees to generate power. Becker did something else.

He stuck out his butt. Like, really stuck it out. His serve motion involved this pronounced posterior thrust that helped him achieve that devastating topspin and pace.

I went to the bedroom mirror. Turned sideways. Attempted the same motion. And that’s when it hit me with all the force of one of Becker’s 130-mph serves: I didn’t have a butt to stick out. Where Becker had curvature, I had...continuity. My back just sort of continued into my legs.

Reality set in. No bum-bum, no boom-boom.

By high school, I had pivoted to more cerebral pursuits like creative writing and a burgeoning interest in art history. Art history seemed safe—just looking at old paintings and Roman sculptures, right? Wrong. It became immediately clear that every civilization in human history had one thing in common: great asses.

One fateful morning during my sophomore year, I was standing at my locker wearing a pair of saggy-bottomed Gap khakis when I heard giggles behind me. The distinctive sound of teenage girls discovering something hilarious and vaguely cruel.

“He has no butt!” another voice announced with the confidence of a scientist making a groundbreaking discovery. More giggling. Then came the death blow that would echo through the halls of my high school for the next two and a half years:

Sure, it wasn’t the most creative nickname, but it had just enough alliteration to spread like a pre-internet meme. From that day on, I knew “they” knew.

I RECENTLY TURNED 50. I’m happily married and have two amazing kids. Life is good, but I’d be lying if I said the ghost of No-Butt Bender doesn’t haunt me. As I’ve aged, what little I had has somehow continued to erode. My wife loves me despite my anatomical deficiency but remains perpetually baffled by the lack of rear topography. And what was once giggles from teenage girls is now full-throated hysterical laughter from my 13- and 11-year-old children when they catch a glimpse of me in anything form-fitting.

Recently, Instagram’s insecurity algorithm served me up an ad for Perk men’s clothing. Their website is a gallery of flattering middle-aged bum-shots, but what really stopped my scroll was the pitch for their pants: “Our chinos magically turn even a flat butt into a compliment magnet. Whether you’re 25 or 65, that never gets old!”

You have to understand—when you have no butt, no pants are flattering. So when Perk starts advertising pants that promise to be so bootylicious you’ll receive compliments everywhere you go, I dared to dream. I did what any man starved for posterior validation does—panic-ordered chinos in every damn color.

The moment the package arrived, I tore it open. I slipped them on, turned toward the mirror, and—holy hell—was that…something? An actual curve? A protrusion? I squinted. I turned. And I practically sprinted to the living room where the family was waiting and struck a pose.

Crickets. Then a full collapse into hysterical laughter.

“I’m sorry,” my wife gasped between laughs, “but those pants are working so hard and getting nowhere.”

I was tired of feeling like the only person other than Hank Hill to be cursed with diminished gluteal syndrome (DGS). It’s a very isolating condition. But I figured if Perk could build an entire company around flat-figured men, there had to be others out there, living their lives in quiet resignation.

Close to midnight on a Wednesday, my face lit only by the glow of my computer, I did the obvious: Googled “men with no butts.”

The search results led me to a Reddit post titled, “Gentlemen, how do you live with having no butt?” This guy laid it all out: approaching 29, finally accepting his glute-free reality, enduring constant jokes from his girlfriend, the whole nine yards of tush-less trauma. But what really got me was his closing line: “Please help.”

I stared at those two words. They contained decades of my own experience—the loneliness, the confusion, the desperation. This post was five years old, but I didn’t care. I had to reach out.

I woke the following morning to find his response waiting like a message from a long-lost brother: “You seem legit I guess. Sure why not.” We were already bonding!

I asked Ross (names have been changed to protect the in-ass-cent) what motivated him to publicly post about his crisis on Reddit, and he said, “It was a genuine problem and if someone’s got an answer that helps, I was going to be all ears.”

Ross first became aware of his DGS when a friend at the gym dropped the bomb: “Dude, you have no ass whatsoever.” The keen observation sent Ross into a spiral of photographic evidence review. He started examining pictures of himself and came to the only possible conclusion: “Huh, I guess I don’t.” It was his own bedroom-mirror moment.

As for the wisdom he received from the Reddit hivemind, he said it was mostly people prescribing the usual suspects: sprints and squats: “I was doing a shit ton of those back then and still do,” he told me. Five years later, he reports modest gains: “It’s no dump truck but I’ve got a little bit of cushion for comfort back there.” He eventually broke up with the ass-teaser (good riddance) and has been dating a new lady who actually pinches it every now and then—a gesture that, for men like us, feels like winning the Mega Millions.

But the struggle continues. Despite his Stairmaster devotion, sweatpants still “kind of sag” making it look like he may have taken a small dump in them. Still, Ross gave me the faintest glimmer that improvement was possible. Maybe a guy with no butt could transform it into a bona fide ba-donk-a-donk.

I phoned two lifelines from my past—Jonathan Botts, MD, a college friend who’d become an orthopedic surgeon at the Los Angeles Orthopedic Institute in Sherman Oaks, California, and Derek DeGrazio, Britney Spears’s former physical trainer, who had whipped me into the best shape of my life for my long-ago wedding and now is a partner at Barry’s Bootcamp Miami.

Dr. Botts (who inexplicably escaped high school without a single butt-related nickname) broke down the anatomy: the butt consists of three muscles—the gluteus maximus, medius, and minimus—but it’s the maximus that does the heavy lifting in filling out a man’s pants.

When I asked about the possibility of maximizing my non-existus, he didn’t sugarcoat it. “Here’s the problem: You can’t go from what you are now to just working on your butt and developing a huge butt,” he told me. “People are doing lots of protein intake and protein shakes and bulking up like that. You could get bigger and your butt would get bigger with you. Muscularly. But I don’t know. I don’t think you could take huge leaps with just doing squats and lunges.”

So basically, I’d need to become the Hulk to grow a butt.

Derek offered an alternate strategy with what he calls the Bender Big Three™. “I can’t say for certain, but I think No-Butt Bender can become Mild-Butt Bender with squats for foundation, Romanian deadlifts to pop your glute a little bit, and a nice hip thrust,” he said.

Mild-Butt Bender. It did have a certain je ne sais quoi.

Derek, who freely admits he was born with “a great Italian ass,” is my gluteal bizarro from an alternate universe where only perfect butts exist. He told me his seat meat gave him confidence in high school, something I could only imagine by taping Springsteen’s Born in the USA album cover to the back of my pants. Derek played baseball, a sport notorious for its revealing tight pants. “I don’t know if they came to watch baseball or if they came to watch us in our uniforms,” he recalled, misty-eyed.

I hated him. I loved him. I wanted to be him.

But then—plot twist!—just as I began fantasizing about playing shortstop, Derek dropped a bomb: “I started to realize that what was a blessing at one point became also my Achilles heel at the same time. If there is a downside, I wasn’t the fastest guy on the team.”

Wait. There was a downside to having a butt?

Dr. Botts, who describes his own posterior as “bigger than average,” delivered the final revelation: Having a bigger butt doesn’t automatically mean you have a stronger butt. “If you have a small butt, does that mean you have weak glutes? It doesn’t at all. You can have a big butt and have really weak glutes.”

All these years, I’d associated my small butt with weakness. With inadequacy. With being less than. What if I was wrong? What if, beneath my perpetually drooping pants lurked a superhero-strength posterior more powerful than even Derek’s celebrated Italian ass?

Unlikely. And who am I kidding? It’s not like if I had said to those girls in high school, “Oh no, you don’t understand—it’s really strong!” they would have stopped laughing and made out with me.

But hey, this new knowledge gives me something to cling on to. Wish I could say the same for my pants. Still, I am what I am. I am No-Butt Bender. And if that makes my kids laugh and my wife give me a warm, “It’s okay. I love you anyway” hug, I’ll take it.

#4913. Bitcoin briefly drops to $17K on Kraken, exchange upholds trades

Kraken said all orders were processed correctly and found no evidence of a security breach or compromised accounts.

Crypto exchange Kraken said it would honor trades executed during a sharp selloff across its Canadian-dollar markets that briefly pushed Bitcoin to the equivalent of about $17,000 on Saturday.

In a statement, Kraken said its Canadian-dollar trading pairs experienced heavy selling pressure starting around 20:00 UTC on Friday, causing prices to fall significantly below those on other markets.

The exchange said every order was processed correctly and its security teams found no evidence of a breach or compromised accounts. “Executed trades stand,” Kraken said.

Kraken’s BTC/CAD pair fell to $24,250 Canadian during the 15-minute interval beginning at 08:00 UTC on Saturday, according to exchange trading data.

On Friday, the Bank of Canada’s reference rate showed C$1.4325 per US dollar, putting Bitcoin’s low on Kraken’s Canadian-dollar market at about $16,900. This was about 71% below Bitcoin’s price of around $57,650 on Kraken’s BTC/USD market during the same five-minute interval.

Blockstream CEO Adam Back suggested that a market maker on Kraken’s USD/CAD spot market may have exhausted its funds, causing prices to diverge from broader foreign exchange rates.

Back said arbitrage subsequently spread the pricing distortion to other Canadian-dollar trading pairs. Kraken has not confirmed Back’s proposed explanation.

Cointelegraph reached out to Kraken for comments but did not receive a response before publication.

#4912. Kalshi halts operations in Utah while dodging immediate ban in New York

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Kalshi said it 'made the difficult decision' to pause its operations in Utah after a federal judge allowed the state to enforce its gambling laws.

Kalshi has suspended operations in Utah after a federal judge allowed the state to enforce its gambling laws, while avoiding an immediate ban in New York as its court denied the state's request to shut down the prediction market platform.

A Kalshi spokesperson told The Block on Monday that the company "made the difficult decision" to pause its operations in Utah while it awaits a court decision on the Commodity Futures Trading Commission's request to block the state from enforcing against Kalshi.

"This is an unfortunate outcome for the more than 177,000 Utahns who use Kalshi," said McGavick. "We will continue to fight this in court, and we look forward to returning to Utah once our legal position is vindicated."

The suspension comes amid mounting nationwide legal pressure, as federal regulators and state authorities remained locked in escalating battles over who has the authority to regulate prediction market platforms. The CFTC has maintained that it has exclusive jurisdiction over such platforms under the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA), while several states have argued that certain event contracts violate state gambling laws.

Specifically in Utah, U.S. District Judge Robert J. Shelby ruled in August that the CEA does not preempt the state's anti-gambling laws against Kalshi's event contracts, rejecting the company's claim of federal preemption. Kalshi later appealed, but the federal court of appeals denied its request to block Utah's enforcement in September.

Last month, the CFTC filed a lawsuit against Utah and requested the court to temporarily block the state from enforcing its gambling laws against CFTC-regulated prediction markets, including Kalshi. Judge Shelby has not yet ruled.

Meanwhile in New York, a judge has denied Attorney General Letitia James' request to shut down Kalshi, allowing the prediction market platform to continue operating in the state for now.

During a hearing on Monday, New York Supreme Court Justice Melissa Crane rejected the state's bid for a temporary restraining order (TRO) that seeks to block Kalshi from operating. Kalshi has previously said the potential TRO could shut it down nationwide, though James said that was not her goal.

According to a series of X posts from gaming lawyer Daniel Wallach, the judge said she was uncomfortable granting relief this early in the case, particularly given the scope of the proposed injunction.

"The judge denied the state's request for a TRO, recognizing that there are complex questions about derivatives and preemption," said Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana. "We remain committed to working with the state on a path forward that works for our 1.5 million customers and for the state of New York."

The CFTC and the Department of Justice also sued New York in April, arguing state enforcement actions against prediction market operators are preempted by federal law.

In recent weeks, the CFTC and the DOJ have filed lawsuits against several states, including Utah, Arkansas, and Louisiana, seeking to block state enforcement against prediction market platforms.

#4911. Signal Calls OS-Level AI an 'Existential Threat' and Blocks Screenshots to Fight It

Signal Calls OS-Level AI an 'Existential Threat' and Blocks Screenshots to Fight It

Signal is moving to wall off its messages from the AI assistants now baked into smartphones, restricting screenshots so that systems like Google’s Gemini and Apple’s Siri can’t quietly scan and store users’ private conversations.

The encrypted-messaging nonprofit said Thursday it will block the latest agentic AI features built into iOS 27 and, on Android, automatically enable a “screen security” setting whenever it detects an AI assistant trying to read a user’s Signal screen.

The move extends a tactic Signal first used last year against Microsoft’s Recall, the Windows feature that captured periodic screenshots; the company blocked it to keep Recall from storing images of Signal messages by default.

The concern, Signal says, is that operating-system AI agents work by “scanning” whatever is on a user’s screen, which means Signal data gets captured and handed to those systems to process.

Once that happens, the content lands in whatever local database or remote server the AI is using, outside Signal’s control and beyond the encryption guarantees that have defined the app for a decade.

Users who still want to take screenshots can disable the protection in Signal’s settings, though the company warns that doing so could expose their messages, and their contacts’ messages, to the AI agents.

Signal also conceded the fix is incomplete: It doesn’t stop sneakier methods of access, such as pulling data straight from a device’s filesystem, and it can’t yet be implemented on macOS, where Signal says Apple lacks the documented tools to block screenshots reliably.

Signal also flagged third-party agents like Meta’s Muse, warning they exploit accessibility permissions meant to help people, not to serve as “a skeleton key” for AI.

The worry isn’t hypothetical: A journalist found that Muse had accessed his private iMessages despite his settings and then gave a false explanation of how it knew their contents, an episode that crystallized fears about how much agents can reach once granted deep device permissions.

Signal’s move lands amid a broader reckoning over AI agents accessing data they shouldn’t, from rogue agents breaching systems to banks scrambling to keep autonomous software out of sensitive accounts.

#4909. The future of onchain FX could start with users, not institutional liquidity

Onchain FX does not need to recreate the trillion-dollar institutional market to become useful. Real demand can make smaller markets work first.

Opinion by Michael Egorov, founder of Curve Finance and Yield Basis

For a long time, the obvious way to think about onchain foreign exchange (FX) was to start at the top. Banks tokenize currencies, large financial institutions move their FX activity onchain and institutional liquidity naturally follows.

The end result? Blockchain-based FX eventually grows large enough to resemble its traditional counterpart. But that’s not necessarily how it is going to happen.

In fact, there are growing signs that onchain FX will develop in the exact opposite way — from the bottom up.

Stablecoins like Brazilian Digital (BRZ) or Monerium’s EURe can be quickly redeemed onchain and easily moved from digital wallets to bank accounts. They generate healthy trading activity as decentralized finance (DeFi) users swap them in decentralized exchange (DEX) pools.

Markets like this end up developing in real time around a practical user need, which is moving directly between digital currencies and fiat.

This is still just an early-stage example, but the use case and demand are real enough. Keyrock data shows that the majority of onchain stablecoin trades tied to emerging market currencies fall in the $1,000 to $10,000 range — modest amounts compared to institutional scale — but that’s precisely the point.

It shows how the market is building in the general direction of onchain FX to meet practical payment needs, not just large traders.

People and businesses need to move their money between currencies, and clearly, they are not content to wait for major traditional finance (TradFi) institutions to move their FX operations onto blockchain rails.

Related: Paraguay bank collapse shows stablecoins can offer a lifeline... of sorts

There’s a pretty common assumption about onchain FX in that a market needs huge liquidity before it can become really useful. In practice, though, that’s not entirely true.

If there is genuine demand from someone who needs to exchange dollars into euros, Swiss francs or another currency, even a smaller pool can process those transactions and serve a real economic purpose.

Stablecoin DEX Trade Volume. Source: Keyrock Research

Onchain FX doesn’t need to become big before it becomes useful; instead, it can grow precisely because people are actually using it.

As a matter of fact, this is more or less what happened with stablecoins. They began as a relatively niche crypto-native use case, and yet today we already know them to be part of a much broader financial infrastructure.

Chainalysis recently shared data that shows cross-border stablecoin activity jumped to over $220 billion by mid-2026. That’s an almost 80% growth over the course of one year, proving that demand and activity are constantly on the rise. There is no reason onchain FX can’t follow a similar path.

This is actually the most practical side of this issue. Onchain FX obviously won’t be able to compete with the entire global foreign exchange market anytime soon; that’s still years away. But the more interesting question is what problems it can solve for someone who already has money onchain.

Today, you can exchange a USD stablecoin into a EUR stablecoin (or some other alternative) onchain and use that money for payments with relatively little trouble. In fact, doing it this way is often faster and cheaper than going with a classic international bank transfer.

Related: Circle’s ARC token sale lifts earnings as lower yields weigh on USDC income

The same logic applies to onchain FX. A fintech or crypto-card company does not need to recreate the entire institutional market; what they need is to move their customers’ funds efficiently between currencies.

When someone is trying to pay in a different currency, converting it can add costs to the operation, since it usually involves Visa or Mastercard, which charge around 1% in processing fees.

Onchain exchanges are a much better option here. If user funds are already living on blockchain rails, swapping USD stablecoins into EUR stablecoins can be done directly, without going through any extra intermediaries, which is both faster and less costly.

Banks will most likely remain important entry and exit points where digital money needs to interact with the real world. Issuing and redeeming local currencies, accessing bank accounts, dealing with compliance and such is where banks will take the lead.

But when the money is actually onchain, many of the traditional “in-between” steps can be dropped for greater efficiency.

From the perspective of a user who just wants to conduct a financial transfer with minimal complications, that’s a highly positive improvement. How big or small the overall market matters little in this case.

Real demand is crucial, but it does not magically solve the problem of liquidity. The challenge is not simply to create a EUR/USD or CHF/USD pool; it’s to make them economically sustainable.

This is where automated market makers can make a difference. Traditional market-making firms may not find it attractive to provide liquidity for a small FX market; there may simply not be enough volume to justify the operational costs.

Cross-border stablecoin flows. Source: Chainalysys

Automated liquidity changes that equation because software can provide liquidity continuously without requiring a market-making firm to manually manage every trade.

Imagine two dollar stablecoins, USDC (USDC) and Tether (USDT). From the perspective of a traditional exchange, moving from one dollar stablecoin into another might look like a fairly boring business. Both assets are supposed to be worth roughly $1, so there isn’t much price movement to trade around.

But if there is still a real need for people to switch between stablecoins like that, an automated market maker can create a pool where users can make that swap directly. Each trade generates a small fee for the liquidity providers, and when enough people need the same conversion, a market can exist even when it doesn’t look particularly exciting to a traditional market maker.

This kind of adoption cannot exactly be called explosive, true, but there’s also nothing wrong with slow, natural growth. Sometimes, new markets just need a small group of people who have a problem the existing infrastructure does not solve particularly well. If those users can make the market economically viable, liquidity can grow from there.

About the author: Michael Egorov is the founder of Curve Finance and Yield Basis. He is also a physicist, entrepreneur and crypto maximalist. Michael transitioned from the scientific field to the crypto industry and holds a bronze medal from the International Physics Olympiad and a PhD in ultracold atoms.

#4908. The Best Barbour Jacket Alternatives, According to Fashion Editors

You don’t need a designer price tag to pull off one of menswear’s most iconic outerwear styles.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I’M A SUCKER for a good Barbour jacket. I’ve always considered the British heritage brand’s waxed-cotton outerwear the perfect finishing touch to a nicely put-together fall-winter outfit. Founded in 1894, Barbour originally made weather-resistant jackets for sailors, fishermen, and outdoor workers before becoming a staple of British countryside dressing. Over the years, its association with the royal family, including Princess Diana and King Charles III, helped give the rugged outerwear an upper-class appeal. But in the past five years, Barbour has seen a major resurgence, thanks to the barn-jacket boom on runways at Prada, Loewe, and Bottega Veneta, along with a growing appetite for timeless pieces over fast fashion. Buzzy collaborations with designers like Gucci and Paul Smith, plus the popularity of British countryside style in shows like The Crown, have also introduced the look to a whole new generation of guys.

But as much as I love Barbour jackets, dropping several hundred dollars on one isn’t exactly in everyone’s budget. Some of the brand’s most iconic styles, including the Bedale, Beaufort, and Ashby, will set you back anywhere from $415 to $450. And that’s before you dive into the designer collabs. Take the latest J.Crew x Barbour Beaufort jacket. It’s a fresh, reimagined take on the brand’s signature outerwear, dressed in a bold apple-green shade with a weather-resistant finish and tartan lining. Personally, it has me in a chokehold. At nearly $500, it’s a pretty steep investment, especially if you’re just looking to dip your toes into the barn-jacket trend.

Thankfully, you don’t have to spend that kind of money to capture the same British countryside charm. Plenty of menswear brands have put their own spin on the classic barn jacket, offering similar silhouettes, canvas fabrics, and utilitarian details at more digestible prices. I’ve rounded up some stylish Barbour jacket alternatives from brands like Bonobos and Quince that look just as sharp, whether you’re getting dressed up or keeping it casual.

Of all the Barbour jacket alternatives I’ve tested, Quince’s version comes close in comparison to the Barbour x Paul Smith collaboration I already own. That jacket runs north of $800, while Quince’s costs just over a hundred bucks but with nearly all the same bells and whistles: a two-tone brown colorway, contrasting corduroy collar, and snap-button storm throat flap that helps seal out drafts of wind. There’s also a two-way zipper that lets me open the jacket from the bottom when I need a little more breathing room.

It’s made from 100% organic cotton and fully lined with soft cotton flannel, which adds a welcome layer of warmth on chilly days when I can skip wearing a bulky sweater underneath. The cut hits just below the waist, with a roomy fit that leaves plenty of space for layering without feeling overly baggy. I’ve even worn it through a few light rain showers, and the fabric didn’t get soaked. I also appreciate how the classic design makes it easy to dress up with trousers and an Oxford shirt or keep things casual with jeans and boots.

Uniqlo makes one of the best affordable Barbour alternatives I’ve come across, and at just $80, it looks and feels more expensive than it actually is. The lightly padded cotton-nylon fabric provides just enough warmth for temps below 60 degrees. What I especially like is the clean, sleek design that comes with a concealed front closure that hides the buttons and zipper, giving the jacket a sharper, more polished look when fully fastened. There’s also a classic checked lining and two-way flap pockets that can be accessed from the top or sides.

Fit-wise, it has a relaxed, boxy silhouette that hits right at the waist and leaves enough room to comfortably layer a chunky knit over a dress shirt. And unlike some barn jackets that lean heavily into the rugged workwear aesthetic, this one feels a little more understated. It comes in versatile neutrals like olive, khaki, black, and dark brown, so finding one that works with your wardrobe shouldn’t be difficult.

If you’re looking for something that puts a slightly different spin on the traditional barn jacket, Bonobos has you covered. Its Canvas Ranch Jacket trades the usual waxed-cotton construction for a sturdy cotton-elastane blend that has a little stretch, so I’m not feeling boxed in. But what really caught my attention are the angled patch pockets across the front. They’re a subtle departure from the usual straight-cut or flap pockets you see on most field jackets, giving the whole design a more distinctive, modern feel.

The cut extends past the waist and lands around the upper thigh, offering a little more coverage than your typical trucker jacket. I also like that Bonobos keeps the color options simple with midnight navy and forest green, both of which go nicely with denim, chinos, or tailored trousers.

For guys who want the classic Barbour look with a little more protection against the elements, consider this insulated version from L.L.Bean. Unlike some of the lighter options on this list, this one is packed with 60 grams of PrimaLoft Black insulation, a lightweight synthetic fill that helps keep it toasty inside without the bulk of a heavy winter coat. The 100% cotton exterior has a water-resistant waxed finish that will keep you dry in light rain. It also has a quilted exterior that gives the traditional field-jacket silhouette a slightly more outdoorsy vibe.

It’s slightly fitted, so guys who are super jacked might have to size up. But the regular-length cut makes it easy to wear over everyday layers and not feel overly snug. This is a great option for construction laborers, landscapers, and other guys who spend long hours outdoors. It’s more practical than fashion-forward, but you get the timeless look of a heritage field jacket with added insulation and weather protection that make it useful beyond just completing an outfit.

Flint and Tinder’s waxed trucker jacket famously appeared on Pedro Pascal in HBO’s The Last of Us, and we’ve been major fans ever since. This is a burly, no-nonsense trucker jacket made from Martexin waxed sailcloth that’s treated on both sides to help block wind and repel light rain. The antique-finish metal buttons and earthy color options give it that rugged, worn-in look that only gets better with age. On the inside, a full-length blanket-style lining keeps you cozy during 50-degree mornings.

One thing worth mentioning is that the waxed fabric feels pretty stiff at first, so don’t expect it to have that soft, broken-in feel right out of the gate. But that’s also part of the appeal of this type of outerwear. After three wears, the fabric gradually softens and develops more character. Compared to Barbour’s longer field jackets, this one has a more traditional trucker silhouette, so I can pair it with jeans, boots, and other casual staples.

Not every Barbour alternative needs to look like it belongs in the English countryside. Case in point: AYR’s The Club, which takes a more refined approach. It’s made from weatherproofed waxed cotton that offers a water-repellent finish. The fabric is also light enough to wear across three seasons, making it a versatile option for those in-between days when a heavy coat feels like overkill.

I can throw it over a tee with jeans for the weekend or wear it with a collared shirt and trousers to the office. I wore it in light showers, and it didn’t soak through to my clothes. And at a little over $300, it’s certainly not the cheapest alternative on this list. Still, if you’re after something that feels more stylish than rugged, this one makes a strong case for itself.

#4905. Will Bitcoin’s big rebound hold? More tampered Ledgers found: Hodler’s Digest

Bitcoin rebounds sharply from its new cycle low, but is $55,111 really the bottom? Ledger investigates new reports of tampered devices in Berlin and the UAE.

Bitcoin saw a sharp recovery on the weekend to $61,034, marking an 11% rebound from its new cycle low around $55,111 on Coinbase. But will it hold?

It wasn’t a crypto only crash however, as concerns grow over the sustainability of the debt-financed AI build out. The tech-heavy Nasdaq fell another 5.3% on Friday and is on the verge of a bear market, having dropped almost 20% from its October peak. The S&P 500 fell 8.6% last week, for the 12th worst week since 1952, and gold lost another 4.3% in the past 24 hours, to be 21.6% down since September.

At the time of writing Bitcoin’s price was holding steady around $60,750 but there are plenty of predictions it has further to fall. Many point to historical parallels with previous four year cycles, which bottomed after the US mid-terms.

Earlier in the week veteran trader Peter Brandt noted that:

The future arrived early on Kraken’s Canadian dollar markets when the BTC price briefly crashed to around US$17,000. Despite the price being 71% below its USD market, Kraken said everything was fine, every order was processed correctly and its security teams found no evidence of a breach or compromised accounts. “Executed trades stand,” Kraken said.

Crypto influencer Cryptobull offered a tiny bit of hopium when he pointed out that Bitcoin’s Market Value to Realized Valu (MVRV) peak this cycle only reached 2.29 times above its cost basis, and if you extrapolate that to the cycle low, it would come in at around 1.03 times the cost basis of $53,700. Which would be almost exactly the $55,130 price that Bitcoin just hit.

Unfortunately, cycle theorist Benjamin Cowen says that in bear markets Bitcoin usually ends up much lower than the realized price — and drops down to the balanced price, which Coinglass puts at $38.7K.

Ledger has warned customers who purchased hardware wallets in Berlin since January 2026 to contact its support team as the company investigates reports of tampered devices in Germany and the United Arab Emirates.

Several suspicious devices have been reported in Berlin, while two customers in Dubai reported losing a combined $604,000 in cryptocurrency.

Ledger said the incidents were connected to the same distribution-channel tampering campaign first investigated in Southeast Asia in October. Ledger is also offering physical inspections, including X-ray analysis, at its factory in Vierzon, France, for customers concerned their devices may have been compromised.

Last week Ethlabs was pushing to make Ethereum post quantum and AI proof in 2027. This week’s All Core Devs Call suggested the chain may not even see Glamsterdam activated until May 2027.

However the devs sound as if they’re leaning towards testing it out without making any changes to the gas repricing Ethereum Improvement Proposals (EIPs), and then activating it in mid-January to give companies and projects time to upgrade affected contracts. They punted a decision until next week about testing the fork on Hoodi in December.If they do decide to make modifications to the gas pricing EIPs activation could be pushed until late February or early March, or even April or May.

The call also discussed the possibility of modifying the EIPs in the Hegota fork to prioritize post quantum features, although several devs argued that reducing the upgrade’s scope would offer little benefit unless it also shortened the rollout timeline.

Wallets linked to the Binance hack moved nearly $600 million in Ether over two days, with some of the stolen funds routed through cross-chain swap protocols, according to blockchain analysts.

The attackers moved about $248 million ETH to new wallets on Thursday and another $346 million ETH, on Friday. Attackers routed part of the funds through cross-chain protocols THORChain and Chainflip, according to blockchain data provider Onchain Lens.

THORChain’s 24-hour swap volume soared to $239 million on Friday, pushing the protocol’s earnings to $243,000, according to THORChain’s dashboard.

Retail investors traded $772 million in Anthropic shares on its Nasdaq debut Thursday, but their net purchases totaled just $60 million, about half the $118 million they put into SpaceX on its first day of trading in June.

Anthropic’s first-day retail trading volume topped SpaceX’s $669 million and was the highest for any market debut in Vanda Research’s data, which go back to 2012, Yahoo Finance reported.

Volume counts both purchases and sales, while net buying measures how much purchases exceeded sales.

ANT closed its first day of trading up 17% on the IPO price, but the stock fell to just 7% above the IPO price on day two amid a broad market sell off.

At the end of the week, Bitcoin (BTC) is down 6.5% to trade at $60,815, Ethereum (ETH) is down 5.9% to trade at $1,713 and XRP (XRP) is down 4.3% to $0.02. The total market cap is at $2.08 trillion according to CoinMarketCap.

The top three altcoin losers of the week are Sky (SKY) which was down 22.6%, Artificial Superintelligence Alliance (FET) down 18.4% and Aptos (APT) down 16.8%.

Bitcoin carry trades could regain their edge over US Treasury bills in 2027 as demand for leverage recovers, according to digital asset bank Sygnum.

In its 2027 outlook, it forecasts that the annualized premium on three-month Bitcoin futures at the Chicago Mercantile Exchange would average more than 2% above three-month US Treasury bill yields next year.

Fabian Dori, chief investment officer at Sygnum, told Cointelegraph that returns could recover as investors become more willing to take risks and fewer hedge funds compete for the same profits.

Senator Ruben Gallego says the crypto industry put too much money into Republican election campaigns.

The International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) warned that upgrading blockchains to withstand quantum attacks could slow transactions and raise fees.

In a report released Thursday, IOSCO said some quantum-resistant digital signatures take up far more space than those used to authorize transactions today. Larger signatures mean fewer transactions can fit in each block, which could increase congestion, push up fees and require more storage.

IOSCO cited an experiment in which a test version of Solana ran about 90% slower with quantum-resistant signatures.

The US Senate passed legislation Thursday that would prohibit federally regulated prediction markets from offering contracts tied to war, terrorism, assassination and events primarily determined by a person’s death, sending the bill to the House for consideration.

The DEATH BETS Act, introduced by Democratic Sen. Adam Schiff in March, passed by voice vote on Thursday after lawmakers adopted an amendment by unanimous consent.

Phylax’s system lets decentralized protocols block potentially harmful transactions, but raises questions about potential censorship and Ethereum’s concentrated builder market.

As geopolitical and technological threats multiply, the big question is whether you can hold on to your wealth and access funds when the system fails.

#4902. Fraudsters Used Fake Crypto Exchanges to Steal $37 Million, SEC Alleges

Fraudsters Used Fake Crypto Exchanges to Steal $37 Million, SEC Alleges

The Securities and Exchange Commission has charged eight entities with running a sprawling scheme that used fake crypto trading platforms and sham investment advisers to steal more than $37 million from U.S. investors.

The case targets eight Colorado-registered entities that the SEC says were likely operated by individuals overseas.

According to the complaint, filed in Colorado federal court, the defendants lured victims into believing they were working with a legitimate investment adviser and crypto trading platform, when both were entirely fake, “simply fronts to steal investor money.”

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The mechanics will sound familiar to anyone who has followed the rise of “pig butchering” scams. The SEC said the fraudsters advertised on social media to funnel investors into group and private chats, where operators posing as advisers first coached them to trade stocks on real U.S. markets before steering them toward a bogus crypto platform and pressing them to send as much money as possible. Victims believed they were buying real crypto assets; in reality, the funds were stolen.

To fake legitimacy, the operators went to unusual lengths, the SEC said, fabricating phony SEC certificates and even making filings with the SEC, FINRA and FinCEN, then pointing skeptical investors to those documents as proof they were real.

The scheme ran from August 2024 through May 2026 across four adviser-and-platform pairs, with the perpetrators abandoning one pair and spinning up the next every few months.

“The SEC continues to seek out and expose fraudulent investment scams that prey on U.S. investors,” said Laura D’Allaird, chief of the agency’s Cyber and Emerging Technologies Unit, urging the public to keep reporting such schemes.

The charged entities, carrying officious-sounding names like Convergent Wealth Advisors and Essential Crypto Exchange, face allegations of violating federal antifraud provisions, with several also accused of breaching the Investment Advisers Act.

The SEC is seeking injunctions, disgorgement and civil penalties. It credited cooperation from regulators in Hong Kong, Singapore, China and Switzerland, underscoring the cross-border nature of the fraud.

The case reflects a persistent threat, as fake platforms and social-engineering scams continue to drain billions from victims worldwide.

Chainalysis and others have documented how such operations, often run by organized crime from Southeast Asia, have scaled with the help of AI-generated personas and scripts, turning romance and investment cons into one of crypto’s costliest categories of fraud.

#4899. Bitcoin slips back toward $60,000 as Jensen Huang’s Nvidia return rally fizzles

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Analysts said Huang's return was a reflex rally that did not resolve the macro headwinds bitcoin is facing as a risk asset.

Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell back toward the $60,000 zone during late evening Monday after briefly surging above $64,000 on news of Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO.

The world's largest cryptocurrency climbed as high as $64,500 shortly after Nvidia's board announced Huang's return, alongside a broader stock market jump and a 31.5% surge in NVDA stocks. This reversed a weekend decline near $55,000 that followed his scandal and resignation earlier this month.

By late Monday, however, bitcoin erased most of the gains from earlier in the day and traded as low as $59,603. It has since recovered partially to trade at $60,443.

Min Jung, associate researcher at Presto Research, described the spike as a reflex rally rather than a shift in fundamentals.

"It squeezed shorts for a few hours and then faded, because the news only fixed a leadership shock at Nvidia," Jung told The Block. “The things actually weighing on bitcoin — a strong dollar, hawkish rate pricing, and steady ETF outflows — haven’t changed.”

Zeus Research Analyst Dominick John similarly attributed the rally to "short covering" that ran into overhead supply once forced buying settled. "The failure to hold these levels suggests that sustained spot demand remains weak, leaving the market vulnerable to further downside," John said.

The analyst warned that a sustained break below $60,000 could open a path toward $58,000 and the recent low of $55,000, while reclaiming the $62,000 to $64,000 zone would improve the near-term outlook.

Analysts largely agreed that Huang's return addresses only a fraction of the headwinds facing bitcoin as a risk asset. Jung cited inflation data, Federal Reserve rate expectations, the dollar, and AI-stock volatility as the dominant drivers, with crypto ETF outflows as a main amplifier. Last week, U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs saw nearly $1.79 billion in net outflows, the largest weekly withdrawal since June.

Traders are now focused on Wednesday's core Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) data, a key inflation indicator, and December's Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) decision.

"With a 25bps hike in December almost fully priced in, I think the market will be particularly sensitive to anything that brings a 50bps hike back into the conversation," ViaBTC Chief Analyst Jeff Ko said. "Within crypto, I would watch whether spot Bitcoin ETF flows turn positive following Huang's return. I am also keeping an eye on USDT, which is still trading at around 0.9987, below the 0.999 level that has marked risk-off phases before."

GTA 6 Leaker Dangles a PC Port Behind a $15 Million Crypto Pump

A notorious Grand Theft Auto 6 leaker is holding a potential PC version of the game hostage to a cryptocurrency payday, promising to release the final file needed to make a leaked build playable once its meme coin hits a $15 million market cap.

The leaker, who goes by Cyberleek, has released what it claims is a near-complete 209 GB Xbox build of GTA 6 from November 2026 via torrent, according to a report from PC Gamer.

The one piece withheld is the main executable, the file that would let modders begin emulating the game on PC, potentially bringing it to the platform long before Rockstar Games intends.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Here’s the crypto catch. Cyberleek says it will release the executable on Nov. 23 regardless, but will drop it early if its token, called CYBERLEEK, reaches a $15 million market cap. That’s half the $30 million target the leaker floated in October.

The coin is down some 20% on the week, but the numbers will fluctuate wildly given the very thin liquidity on a small cap coin.

The setup has drawn open skepticism that it’s a cash grab, or worse. Cyberleek has also claimed to hold a PS5 build as a fallback if the Xbox files can’t be made to work.

The episode sits at the increasingly busy intersection of crypto, piracy, and AI. Modern Xbox games are architecturally closer to PC software than PlayStation titles, and AI coding tools have accelerated the kind of decompilation work that makes fast ports plausible, the same dynamic behind a wave of AI-assisted game ports this year.

It also caps a chaotic launch window for GTA that crypto has repeatedly crashed. One trader banked around $66,000 on a GTA 6 meme coin amid ticker confusion, and the game’s disc-free, digital-only release has fueled broader debate, with GameStop’s CEO arguing that digital-only games have hurt consumers.

None of the leaked files have been independently confirmed as genuine, and there is still no official GTA 6 PC version; Rockstar, famously litigious, could respond with legal action rather than a port.

#4895. Half of wallets on Trump’s dinner list got tokens from non-US exchanges

Public Citizen traced 66 of 130 qualifying wallets to exchanges that restrict US users, raising questions over who is attending Trump’s memecoin dinner on Sunday.

More than half the wallets that qualified for Donald Trump’s memecoin dinner on Sunday received TRUMP tokens from crypto exchanges that bar US users, according to analysis from consumer advocacy group Public Citizen.

Trump is hosting his second dinner for his top memecoin holders at Trump National Golf Club in Sterling, Virginia, more than a year after an inaugural dinner in May 2025. The dinner offers up to 185 seats based on time-weighted TRUMP holdings.

Public Citizen said it traced 66 of the 130 wallets on the public leaderboard to exchanges including Binance (the global exchange), Bybit and BloFin — which are not permitted to serve US citizens. The list also included 15 of the 29 wallets that qualified for a VIP reception with the president.

“If those bans are enforced, many of the people vying for a seat at the president’s table don’t live in the United States,” said Public Citizen research director Zach Everson.

Trump’s memecoin events have drawn scrutiny over whether foreign buyers can essentially buy access to the president. Democratic lawmakers called on the White House to release the guest list for the first dinner in May 2025, while some joined a protest outside the golf club. They renewed the request again in October.

FastX, a Singapore-linked crypto trading platform, was the top-ranked wallet, holding $1.65 million in TRUMP at the time of the snapshot on Nov. 16.

The third highest ranked wallet has been linked to Unitas, a stablecoin issuer run by a British Virgin Islands company, according to Public Citizen.

Morten Christensen, a crypto entrepreneur from the Netherlands and founder and CEO of AirdropAlert, shared a picture from the Trump gala dinner on X. He came 26th with $45,351 in time-weighted TRUMP holdings.

Public Citizen also identified 14 wallets it suspects were controlled by the same person or group. Each received approximately 3,700 TRUMP tokens from Binance in October before being emptied weeks ahead of the cutoff.
